L5: **Table 1. Administrative Application Information**
L6: Category	Application Information
Application type	NDA
Application number(s)	215888
Priority or standard	Priority
Submit date	5/27/2021
Received date	5/27/2021
PDUFA goal date	4/27/2022
Division/office	Division of Anti-Infectives (DAI)
Review completion date	4/25/2022
Established/proper name	Oteseconazole
(Proposed) proprietary name	Vivjoa
Pharmacologic class	Azole antifungal
Code name	VT-1161
Applicant	Mycovia Pharmaceuticals, Inc.
Dosage form/formulation	Capsule
Applicant proposed indication/
population	
Proposed SNOMED indication	SCTID: 708126004
Regulatory action	Approval
Approved dosage	VIVJOA-only dosage regimen:
- On Day 1: Administer VIVJOA 600 mg (as a single dose), then
- On Day 2: Administer VIVJOA 450 mg (as a single dose), then
- Beginning on Day 14: Administer VIVJOA 150 mg once a week (every 7 days) for 11 weeks (Weeks 2 through 12).
Fluconazole/VIVJOA dosage regimen:
- On Day 1, Day 4, and Day 7: Administer fluconazole 150 mg orally, then
- On Days 14 through 20: Administer VIVJOA 150 mg once daily for 7 days, then
- Beginning on Day 28: Administer VIVJOA 150 mg once a week (every 7 days) for 11 weeks (Weeks 4 through 14).
Approved indication/
population	To reduce the incidence of recurrent vulvovaginal candidiasis (RVVC) in females with a history of RVVC who are NOT of reproductive potential
Approved SNOMED term for
indication	Recurrent candidiasis of vagina

L27: ## I. Executive Summary
L28: ## 1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L29: The new drug application (NDA) 215888 for oteseconazole, an azole antifungal drug, was submitted by Mycovia Pharmaceuticals, Inc. The Applicant proposed oteseconazole
L30: (b) (4)
L31: (b) (4)
L32: The NDA was reviewed by the multidisciplinary review team.
L33: (b) (4)
L34: The clinical and statistical reviewers concluded that the Applicant provided insufficient information to demonstrate the efficacy of oteseconazole
L35: (b) (4)
L36: The review team also concluded that ocular abnormalities observed in pre- and postnatal developmental studies along with oteseconazole's prolonged drug exposure window of approximately 690 days (based on 5 times the half-life of oteseconazole) should result in contraindication of oteseconazole for pregnant and lactating females as well as females of reproductive potential. In making their risk-benefit assessments the review team also considered that RVVC is a non-life-threatening disease. Consequently, the team recommended to indicate oteseconazole to reduce the incidence of RVVC in females with a history of RVVC who are not of reproductive potential, and I, the signatory authority for this application, concur with those recommendations.
L37: The Applicant submitted three adequate and well controlled phase 3 trials. The trials consisted of an induction phase to treat an acute episode of RVVC and a maintenance phase. In two trials fluconazole was used in the induction phase followed by an 11-week maintenance phase of either oteseconazole or placebo weekly. In these trials oteseconazole was superior to placebo in the proportion of patients with \ge 1 acute VVC episode through Week 48 post-randomization.
L38: In the third trial, during the induction phase, patients received either fluconazole or oteseconazole followed by a maintenance regimen of either oteseconazole weekly or placebo weekly for 11 weeks. Oteseconazole was superior to fluconazole/placebo in the proportion of patients with \ge 1 acute VVC episode through Week 50 post-randomization.

L49: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L50: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L51: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L52: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Approximately 70 to 75% of all females will experience an infection with vulvovaginal candidiasis (VVC) at least once in their lifetime. Moreover, it is estimated that 40 to 50% of these females will experience a recurrence of VVC (Sobel 2007).
Only a minority of females, approximately 5 to 8%, will experience recurrent vulvovaginal candidiasis (RVVC), defined as ≥3 episodes of Candida vulvovaginitis per year (Sobel 2007; Sobel 2016)
Host, genetic, and behavioral factors all play a role in whether a woman may be more susceptible to developing VVC. These risk factors facilitate the transformation from asymptomatic colonization to symptomatic vaginitis (Sobel 2016).
Of note, at least 20% of all females are colonized vaginally with Candida species. During pregnancy this increases to 30%.
While most females will experience at least one VVC infection in their lifetime, a minority of females (an estimated 5 to 8%) will experience RVVC, defined as ≥3 episodes of Candida vulvovaginitis per year, in their lifetimes.
L53: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current Treatment Options	Uncomplicated VVC is often treated effectively with a short-course of an oral or a topical azole formulation (i.e., single dose and regimens of 1–3 days), which achieves relief of symptoms and negative cultures in 80% to 90% of patients who complete therapy (Workowski et al. 2021).
There are presently no FDA approved therapies for the treatment and/or prevention of RVVC. Most recommended therapies for this condition are prescribed off-label (Sobel et al. 2004; Workowski et al. 2021).
In their 2021 sexually transmitted infections (STI) guidelines, Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) recommends, for the treatment of RVVC, a longer duration initial regimen (e.g., 7-14 day topical therapy or fluconazole every 72 hours for a total of 3 doses) followed by a weekly maintenance regimen of oral fluconazole for 6 months to induce and then maintain mycologic remission (Workowski et al. 2021).
Treatment guidelines, including those from the American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists (ACOG) and the Infectious Diseases Society of America (IDSA), recommend oral or topical agents, typically two agents divided into a short course induction regimen followed by a longer maintenance regimen, for the management of RVVC (The American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists 2006; Pappas et al. 2016).
Studies have demonstrated that even a six-month period of effective suppressive prophylaxis for RVVC does not guarantee a cure during the subsequent six months after suppressive therapy has been discontinued (Sobel et al. 2004).
There are presently no FDA approved therapies for RVVC.
CDC recommends combined induction and maintenance regimens to attempt to achieve mycologic remission.
Even after six months of suppressive prophylaxis, sustained remission of recurrent VVC episodes is difficult to achieve after suppressive therapy has been discontinued (Sobel 2016).
L54: Benefit	
The efficacy of oteseconazole for reducing the incidence of RVVC was evaluated in two randomized, placebo-controlled trials, CL-011 and CL-012. Both trials consisted of two phases: an open-label induction phase and an 11-week maintenance phase. Patients received three sequential doses of 150 mg of fluconazole during the induction phase. Patients returned 14 days after the first dose of fluconazole and if the acute VVC episode was resolved (vulvovaginal signs and symptoms score <3), they were randomized (2:1) to receive either 150 mg of oteseconazole or placebo for 7 days followed by 11 weekly doses in the maintenance phase. Efficacy was assessed by the proportion of patients with \ge 1 culture verified acute VVC episode (positive fungal culture for Candida species associated with a clinical signs and symptoms score of \ge 3) during the maintenance phase through Week 48.
Oteseconazole was superior to placebo in the proportion of patients with \ge 1 culture verified acute VVC episode through Week 48 and the proportion of patients with \ge 1 KOH-verified acute VVC episode through Week 48.
A third trial, CL-017, evaluated the efficacy and safety of oteseconazole versus fluconazole and placebo in postmenarchal females with RVVC who presented with an acute VVC episode. The trial consisted of two phases: an induction and a maintenance phase. Patients received 1050 mg of oteseconazole over two days (600 mg [4x150 mg] on Day 1 and 450 mg [3x150 mg] on Day 2) or three sequential doses of 150 mg of fluconazole (every 72 hours). Patients returned 14 days after the first doses and entered the maintenance phase only if the acute VVC episode was resolved. During the maintenance phase, patients received 150 mg oteseconazole weekly or placebo weekly for 11 weeks. Efficacy was assessed by the proportion of subjects with \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episode during the maintenance phase (post-

Oteseconazole is effective for reducing the incidence of recurrent VVC in postmenarchal patients with a history of RVVC. Two regimens are effective: induction with fluconazole followed by maintenance with oteseconazole, and an oteseconazole-only regimen for both induction and maintenance.
The Applicant provided insufficient information to support indications for oteseconazole (b) (4) Additional placebo-controlled studies will be needed to support these indications.

Oteseconazole was superior to fluconazole/placebo in the proportion of patients with \ge 1 culture verified recurrent acute VVC episode during the maintenance phase (post randomization through Week 50) or who failed clearing their infection during the induction phase.

L56: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and Risk Management	Ocular abnormalities such as cataracts, opacities, and exophthalmos/bupthalmos were observed in the offspring of pregnant rats dosed at 7.5 mg/kg/day during pre- and postnatal developmental studies.
The safety profile of oteseconazole, as demonstrated in three phase 3 clinical trials, was found to be mostly benign. Urinary tract infections, bacterial vaginosis, headache, nausea, abdominal pain, pyrexia and diarrhea occurred in ≥2% of all oteseconazole exposed subjects in the phase 3 trials.
Transient, largely asymptomatic, unexplained elevations in creatine phosphokinase (CPK) of ≥10 x ULN, with no associated symptoms of myalgias, muscle weakness, evidence of myoglobinuria or creatinine elevations were observed in oteseconazole treated subjects while on treatment and up to 5 to 8 months post-therapy (all within 2 half-lives).
Pre-clinical ocular findings found in rat pups along with oteseconazole's prolonged drug exposure window of approximately 690 days (based on 5 times the half-life of oteseconazole) will result in contraindication of this drug for pregnant and lactating females as well as females of reproductive potential.
Headache, nausea and CPK elevations occurred more commonly in the oteseconazole arm than in the comparator/placebo arm and will be described in product labeling.
L57: *(Sobel et al. 2004; Sobel 2007; Kourtis et al. 2014; Sobel 2016; Workowski et al. 2021; The American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists 2006; Pappas et al. 2016)
L58: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L59: Vulvovaginal candidiasis is a common condition. Approximately 70 to 75% of females experience an episode of VVC in their lifetime and 40 to 50% of these females will experience a recurrence. While VVC may result in significant discomfort and affect patients' everyday life, it is not a life-threatening condition and is not usually associated with serious health consequences. VVC is treated with 1 to 3 days of oral or topical azoles and 80% to 90% of patients achieve relief of symptoms. While there are treatments approved for acute VVC, no therapies are approved for reduction of RVVC episodes.
L60: Oteseconazole is an azole antifungal drug that was proposed (b) (4). One of the distinguishing characteristics of oteseconazole is its prolonged drug exposure window of approximately 690 days (based on 5 times the half-life of oteseconazole).
L61: The application was supported by three adequate and well controlled phase 3 trials. The trials consisted of an induction phase to treat an acute episode of RVVC and a maintenance phase. In two trials the acute VVC episode was treated with fluconazole. In the third trial either fluconazole or oteseconazole was used for induction. In all three trials in the maintenance phase patients received either oteseconazole or placebo, and in all three trials oteseconazole was found to be superior to placebo in the proportion of patients with \ge 1 acute VVC episode up to Week 50 post-randomization. The safety profile of oteseconazole in the three phase 3 clinical trials was comparable to the comparator regimens.
L62: However, the evidence demonstrating the efficacy of oteseconazole (b) (4)
L63: (b) (4) was found to be insufficient (b) (4)
L64: (b) (4)
L65: In addition, ocular abnormalities were observed in pre- and postnatal developmental studies. Given that a long drug exposure window of oteseconazole precludes reliable use of contraceptive measures, it was recommended to contraindicate oteseconazole for pregnant and lactating females as well as females of reproductive potential.
L66: Consequently, the recommended indication for oteseconazole is to reduce the incidence of RVVC in females with a history of RVVC who are not of reproductive potential.
L67: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L68: ## 3. Introduction
L69: Oteseconazole (VT-1161, OTE) is an azole antifungal metalloenzyme inhibitor targeting the fungal sterol, 14\alpha demethylase (CYP51), which mediates fungal membrane permeability. The clinical development program for oteseconazole commenced in 2011 and resulted in the May 2021 submission of NDA 215888, which is the subject of this Integrated Review.
L70: The Applicant is seeking approval for the following indications:
L71: (b) (4)
L72: Key regulatory highlights in the oteseconazole development program include: the granting of both Fast Track and Qualified Infectious Disease Product designations for the treatment of VVC and RVVC in September 2016; an End of Phase 2 meeting in August 2017, where the phase 3 development program was discussed; and a type B pre-NDA meeting in November 2020, where key discussion topics for this review such as the pre- and postnatal development animal study findings and the proposed indications were discussed.
L73: Literature estimates indicate that VVC affects between 70 to 75% of females at least once during their lives, with females between the ages of 25 and 34 being the most frequently affected. VVC is characterized by acute itching, vaginal discharge, or symptoms of vaginal irritation, such as burning and soreness. *Candida albicans* accounts for 85 to 95% of all *Candida spp.* isolated from the vagina. Forty to fifty percent of all females will experience a recurrence of VVC and 5 to 8% of all adult females will have RVVC. The CDC defines RVVC as having three or more episodes of VVC in a 12-month period, whereas the Infectious Diseases Society of America (IDSA) and ACOG define RVVC as having four or more episodes of VVC in a 12-month period. For the purposes of this NDA, the CDC definition of three or more episodes of VVC in a 12-month period was adopted (Sobel 2007; Workowski et al. 2021).
L74: Presently, there are no FDA approved therapeutic options for the treatment of RVVC. Recommended regimens include short course oral (e.g., fluconazole) or topical induction regimens (3 to 7 days) for the acute VVC infection, followed by a 6-month maintenance regimen typically given at 1-week intervals. Studies have shown that upon cessation of therapy, "approximately 50% of women will develop a culture positive episode of RVVC within 3 to 4 months" (Sobel 2016; Workowski et al. 2021).
L75: The efficacy and safety of oteseconazole were supported by three phase 3 clinical trials: CL-011, CL-012, and CL-017. Key issues identified during review of this application are listed below and discussed in Sections 6.3 and 7.7.

L84: ## 3.2. Approach to the Review
L85: Table 3 provides an overview of the major clinical trials conducted by the Applicant during the oteseconazole clinical development program. Trials CL-011 and CL-012 provided support for the approval of oteseconazole for the reduction of RVVC using a dosage regimen of induction with fluconazole followed by maintenance with oteseconazole. Trial CL-017 provided support for an alternative dosing regimen using oteseconazole for both induction and maintenance.
L86: The primary sources of safety data were the treatment-emergent adverse events and laboratory data from Trials CL-011, CL-012, and CL-017. The phase 2b dose-finding trial, Trial CL-006, was not analyzed in support of efficacy but was reviewed to supplement safety information. Additional safety data were provided by phase 1 studies and studies for other indications. The 120-day safety update is discussed in Section 7.6.7.3.
L87: The trials submitted by the Applicant were sufficient to establish the safety and efficacy of oteseconazole for reducing the incidence of RVVC in the indicated population. They were not sufficient to support the requested indications [REDACTED] (b) (4)

L101: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L102: Clinical pharmacology properties of oteseconazole were comprehensively evaluated and are summarized in Table 5.
L103: **Table 5. Summary of General Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics**
L104: Characteristic	Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity
Established pharmacologic class (EPC)	Oteseconazole is an azole antifungal
Mechanism of action	Oteseconazole inhibits fungal lanosterol demethylase (CYP51) which catalyzes an early step in the conversion of lanosterol to ergosterol, which is required for fungal cell wall formation. Interruption of this conversion results in depletion of ergosterol, inhibition of fungal cell growth and increased fungal cell wall permeability.
Active moieties	The active moiety is oteseconazole
QT prolongation	At the dose evaluated in the thorough QT study (600 mg on Days 1-13 and 1200 mg on Day 14), which yielded peak exposures ~5 times the Cmax associated with the recommended dosing regimen in the maintenance phase, oteseconazole did not prolong the QT interval to a clinically significant extent. A mean reduction of the QTcF (upper 90% confidence interval) of -5.6 milliseconds (-10.5 milliseconds) was observed and this effect was oteseconazole concentration-dependent.
General Information
Bioanalysis	Validated LC/MS/MS methods were used to quantify oteseconazole concentrations in human plasma, blood, urine, and feces as relevant to individual studies.
Healthy subjects versus patients	The PK of oteseconazole is similar in healthy subjects and subjects with VVC.
Drug exposure following the therapeutic dosing regimen	
PK Parameter	Mean (\pmSD)a
Cmax (\mug/mL)	2.78 (1.25)
AUC24h (h\cdot\mug/mL)	64.2 (29.4)
Cmin (\mug/mL)	2.53 (1.19)
aBased on population PK simulated exposures at Week 13 (end of treatment) in Phase 1 and 2 subjects weighing ~70 kg administered oteseconazole 600 mg on Day 1 followed by 450 mg on Day 2 then 150 mg weekly beginning on Day 14 for 11 weeks.

Range of effective dosage(s) or exposure	The relationships between oteseconazole Day 14 exposure and the endpoint of recurrence of culture-verified acute episodes of VVC in the maintenance phase in Phase 3 trials was flat across the range of oteseconazole Day 14 exposures observed following a regimen of 150 mg QD for 7 days in studies CL-011 and CL-012, and a regimen of 600 mg QD on Day 1 and 450 mg on Day 2 in Study CL-017.
Maximally tolerated dosage or exposure	An MTD was not determined. The highest single and multiple doses evaluated in humans, both of which were evaluated in the TQT trial were 1200 mg and 600 mg daily for 14 days, respectively. The highest dose evaluated in the MAD study was 320 mg QD for 7 days.
L105: Drug Information
Characteristic	
Dosage proportionality	Oteseconazole exposures increased in a dose-proportional manner while C_{max} increased less than dose-proportionally over a dose range of 20 to 320 mg.
Accumulation	Mean accumulation ratio following daily dose administration for 7 days was 4.5 to 6.2 for a dose range of 40 to 320 mg.
Bridge between to-be-marketed and clinical trial formulations	The to-be-marketed formulation was identical to the Phase 3 trial formulation except in appearance of the capsule shell.
Absorption
Bioavailability	Absolute bioavailability of oteseconazole was not determined. Based on the recovery of radiolabeled oteseconazole in the human mass balance study, it is estimated to be ~75%.

Geometric least square mean and 90% CI	A high-fat, high-calorie meal was defined as 900-1000 kcal with 500-600 kcal from fat.
A low-fat, low-calorie meal was defined as 650 kcal with 117 kcal from fat.
Distribution
Volume of distribution	423 L
Plasma protein binding	≥ 99.5% at oteseconazole concentrations of 0.26 – 2.5 µg/mL.
Drug as substrate of transporters	Oteseconazole is not a substrate of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1/B3, OAT1/3 or OCT2 transporters. It is unknown whether oteseconazole is a substrate of the MATE transporter.
Elimination
Mass balance results	Following administration of 600 mg [^{14}C]oteseconazole, 82% of the administered dose was recovered in excreta; 56% was recovered in feces and 26% was recovered in urine. No metabolites were identified.
Clearance	0.253 L/h
Half-life	Median terminal half-life is 138 days (for subject weighing 70 kg)
Metabolic pathway(s)	No oteseconazole metabolites were detected in incubations with pooled human liver microsomes or in human mass balance studies.
Primary excretion pathways (% dosage)	Oteseconazole appears to be primarily excreted by biliary excretion. An estimated 66% of the absorbed dose is excreted in feces, and 34% is excreted in urine.
L106: Drug Information
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	The predicted oteseconazole AUC in subjects weighing <40 kg was up to 2-fold higher than the AUC in subjects with body weight 60 – 70 kg. Because only 1 subject weighing <40 kg (actual weight 36 kg) was identified across Phase 3 clinical studies, the clinical significance of this observation is unknown.
Age	No clinically significant differences in the PK of oteseconazole were observed based on age.
Renal impairment	PopPK analyses indicate that mild to moderate renal impairment (eGFR 30-89 mL/min based on the MDRD equation) does not significantly alter the elimination of oteseconazole. However, use of oteseconazole is not recommended in the setting of severe renal impairment or ESRD due to the potential for increased drug accumulation.
Hepatic impairment	The effect of hepatic impairment on the PK of oteseconazole was not evaluated. Because biliary excretion appears to be the primary elimination pathway for oteseconazole, increased accumulation of oteseconazole may occur in the setting of hepatic impairment. Mild impairment (Child-Pugh A) is not expected to significantly alter the elimination of oteseconazole. However, use of oteseconazole is not recommended in the setting of moderate to severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B-C) due to the potential for increased drug accumulation.
Drug Interaction Liability (drug as perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	In vitro, oteseconazole was an inducer and inhibitor of CYP3A4/5.
In vivo, coadministration of oteseconazole with midazolam (CYP3A4 substrate) did not result in clinically significant inhibition or induction of midazolam metabolism.
Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	In vitro, oteseconazole was an inhibitor of BCRP.
Results of clinical drug-drug interaction studies indicate that oteseconazole had no clinically meaningful effect on the PK of digoxin (P-gp substrate).
Results of clinical drug-drug interaction studies indicate that coadministration of oteseconazole with rosuvastatin (BCRP substrate) resulted in a clinically significant increase in rosuvastatin exposure.
L107: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the curve; AUC24, area under the curve at 24 hours; BCRP, breast cancer resistance protein; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; Cmin, minimum plasma concentration; CYP, cytochrome P450 isoenzyme; eGFR, estimated glomerular filtration rate; ESRD, end stage renal disease; GMR, geometric mean ratio; LC/MS/MS, liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry; MATE, multidrug and toxic compound extrusion; MDRD, modification of diet in renal disease; OATP, organic anion transporting polypeptides; P-gp, P-glycoprotein; PK, pharmacokinetics, TQT, thorough QT.
L108: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L109: ## Clinical Microbiology
L110: ## In Vivo Activity
L111: The activity of oteseconazole was evaluated in two studies using an estrogen-dependent murine model of vaginal candidiasis. In the first study, mice were rendered pseudo-estrus with the administration of estradiol and subsequently infected with a wild-type strain of *C. albicans*. At daily doses of 4, 10, or 25 mg/kg administered by oral lavage for 4 days, oteseconazole treatment yielded &gt;90% reduction in vaginal lavage fluid fungal burden compared to vehicle (1 day following completion of treatment). Additionally, the 25 mg/kg dose of oteseconazole maintained antifungal activity similar to that of fluconazole through 4 days post-completion of treatment. For each dose level, oteseconazole plasma and vaginal tissue levels assessed 4 days after completion of treatment were comparable. Based on these studies, a threshold oteseconazole plasma concentration of \ge 1 \mu\text{g/mL} (which was associated with the 4 mg/kg dose) was used to inform dose selection in clinical efficacy trials.
L112: In a second study conducted in the same murine model of vaginal candidiasis, the activity of oteseconazole and fluconazole were evaluated against 3 clinical isolates resistant to fluconazole (with oteseconazole minimum inhibitory concentrations [MICs] of \le 0.015, 0.12 and 2 \mu\text{g/mL}). Overall, at the same dose level (25 mg/kg daily for 4 days), oteseconazole achieved a greater reduction in fungal burden compared to fluconazole.
L113: ## In Vitro Activity
L114: Oteseconazole demonstrated activity with minimum inhibitory concentration to inhibit growth of 90% of organisms (\text{MIC}_{90}) values ranging from &lt;0.005 to 4 \text{ mcg/mL} against *Candida albicans*, *C. glabrata*, *C. krusei*, *C. parapsilosis*, *C. tropicalis*, *C. lusitaniae*, and *C. auris*. The oteseconazole MIC range and \text{MIC}_{90} against approximately 1433 isolates of *C. albicans* were &lt;0.0005 - &gt;0.0.25 \text{ mcg/mL} and 0.008-0.03 \text{ mcg/mL}, respectively. Against 68 fluconazole resistant isolates, the MIC range and \text{MIC}_{90} were \le 0.015 to &gt;8 \text{ mcg/mL} and 1 \text{ mcg/mL}, respectively. Against approximately 332 isolates of *C. glabrata*, the oteseconazole response MIC range and \text{MIC}_{90} were \le 0.015 to 1 \text{ mcg/mL} and 0.125-1 \text{ mcg/mL}, respectively. Against 58 isolates of *C. parapsilosis*, the MIC range and \text{MIC}_{90} values were \le 0.0005 to 0.12 \text{ mcg/mL} and 0.05 to 0.15 \text{ mcg/mL}, respectively. Against 50 isolates of *C. krusei* the MIC range and \text{MIC}_{90} were \le 0.015 to 1 \text{ mcg/mL} and 0.5 \text{ mcg/mL}, respectively. Against 21 isolates of *C. tropicalis*, the MIC range and \text{MIC}_{90} values were 0.004 to 0.015 \text{ mcg/mL} and 0.06 to 0.15 \text{ mcg/mL}, respectively. Against 15 isolates of *C. lusitaniae*, the MIC range and \text{MIC}_{90} values were 0.01 to 0.25 \text{ mcg/mL} and 0.2 \text{ mcg/mL}, respectively, and against 100 isolates of *C. auris*, the MIC range and \text{MIC}_{90} values were \le 0.015 to &gt;8 \text{ mcg/mL} and 4 \text{ mcg/mL}, respectively.
L115: For the three phase 3 studies, vaginal specimens (swabs) were collected at screening and at all scheduled and unscheduled visits where recurrent vulvovaginal candidiasis was suspected. A potassium hydroxide (KOH) preparation or Gram stain was performed on all specimens at baseline/screening and at selected visits. Specimens for fungal culture were collected and sent to the Central Laboratory (b) (4) for fungal isolation, identification, and
L116: susceptibility testing. Specimens were collected to rule out bacterial vaginosis, *Chlamydia trachomatis*, *Neisseria gonorrhoeae* and *Trichomonas vaginalis*.
L117: In vitro susceptibilities were determined by broth micro dilution methods according to the Clinical and Laboratory standards Institute Standard, Method M27:3 -*Reference Method for Broth Dilution Antifungal Susceptibility Testing of Yeasts; Approved. Standard-4th Edition.*
L118: Studies CL-011, CL-012, and CL-017 were randomized, double-blind, placebo controlled, parallel group studies with an open-label induction phase, and all subjects had a history of RVVC. The subjects from CL-011 and CL-012 were selected from Europe and the United States, while the subjects in CL-017 were selected primarily within the United States. Table 6 depicts the MIC values (range, inhibit growth of 50% [MIC50] and MIC90) of the two most common *Candida* species identified in the three phase 3 studies. In CL-011, the MIC range, MIC50 and MIC90 for *C. albicans* were \le 0.0005 to 0.12 mcg/mL, 0.002 mcg/mL and 0.008 mcg/mL, respectively. The MIC range, MIC50, and MIC90 for *C. glabrata* were 0.002 to 0.125 mcg/mL, 0.015 mcg/mL, and 0.06 mcg/mL, respectively. In CL-012 the range, MIC50, and MIC90 were \le 0.0005 to &gt;0.25 mcg/mL, 0.002 mcg/mL, and 0.03 mcg/mL for *C. albicans*. For *C. glabrata* the range, MIC50, and MIC90 were 0.001 to &gt;0.25 mcg/mL, 0.03 mcg/mL, and 0.125 mcg/mL, respectively. The OTE MIC range, MIC50, and MIC90 for *C. albicans* for all isolates in CL-017 were \le 0.0005 to &gt;0.25 mcg/mL, 0.004 mcg/mL, and 0.06 mcg/mL and the FLU range, MIC50, and MIC90 were \le 0.06 to 16 mcg/mL, 0.25 mcg/mL, and 8 mcg/mL.

L123: ## 6. Assessment of Effectiveness
L124: ## 6.1. Dose and Dose Responsiveness
L125: The Applicant's proposed dosing regimen (b)(4) is as follows:
L126: - Day 1: administer oteseconazole 600 mg (as a single dose) orally
- Day 2: administer oteseconazole 450 mg (as a single dose) orally, then
- Beginning on Day 14: administer oteseconazole 150 mg orally once a week (every 7 days) for 11 weeks (Weeks 2 through 12)
L127: The 2-day starting dose of oteseconazole was only evaluated in one phase 3 trial (CL-017). In the other phase 3 trials (CL-011 and CL-012), treatment was initiated with three doses of 150 mg
L128: fluconazole administered 72 hours apart, followed by a 12-week maintenance phase of oteseconazole as follows:
L129: - Day 1, Day 4, and Day 7: administer fluconazole 150 mg orally
- On Days 14 through 20: administer oteseconazole 150 mg orally once daily for 7 days
- Beginning on Day 28: administer oteseconazole 150 mg orally once a week (every 7 days) for 11 weeks (Weeks 4 through 14)
L130: The 7-day 150 mg daily dosing regimen used in trials CL-011 and CL-012 mirrors the low-dose regimen shown to be efficacious in the phase 2b trial, CL-006. Additionally, this regimen was expected to yield oteseconazole plasma concentrations \ge 1 \mug/mL, which were associated with a significant reduction in fungal burden in the murine model of vaginal candidiasis. Although the 2-day starting dose regimen used in the CL-017 trial was not evaluated in the phase 2 trials, it was predicted (and shown) to achieve Day 14 minimum plasma concentration (C_{min}) values comparable to the 7-day 150 mg daily dosing regimen as summarized in Table 7. Additionally, simulated exposures for the 2-day and the 7-day regimens reflect comparable area under the concentration-time curve during the dosing interval (AUC_{\tau}) at Day 14 (i.e., 234.3 \mug\cdoth/mL versus 236.7 \mug\cdoth/mL, respectively) as shown in Figure 1.
L131: The 150 mg weekly dosing regimen selected for the three phase 3 trials was informed by the pharmacokinetics (PK), efficacy, and safety of oteseconazole in a phase 2b dose-ranging study which evaluated the dosing regimen of 150 mg daily for 7 days, followed by 150 mg weekly for 11 weeks beginning on Day 14 (in addition to 3 other oteseconazole dosing regimens and placebo) for the prevention of recurrence of VVC after induction with fluconazole. The primary endpoint, which was the proportion of subjects with \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episode during the maintenance phase through Week 48 of the study, was statistically significantly lower for the 12-week 150 mg dosing regimen compared to placebo (4.8% versus 24%, p-value &lt;0.0001). Furthermore, the 12-week 150 mg dosing regimen, which was the lowest dose and shortest duration of oteseconazole administration in this study, exhibited comparable efficacy to the higher dose and longer duration dosing regimens.
L132: In the CL-011 and CL-012 trials, fluconazole/oteseconazole was superior to fluconazole/placebo with respect to the proportion of subjects with \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episode through Week 48. Similarly, in Trial CL-017, treatment with the oteseconazole-only regimen was superior to fluconazole/placebo in the proportion of subjects with \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episode through Week 50 (see Section 6.2 for detailed discussion of phase 3 efficacy results).
L133: Based on these results, the review team has proposed the following dosing regimens for the revised indication of reduction of RVVC:
L134: - An oteseconazole-only regimen comprised of:600 mg on Day 1
- 450 mg on Day 2
- 150 mg weekly for 11 weeks beginning on Day 14 post-initiation of treatment
L135: - Or, fluconazole followed by oteseconazole:150 mg fluconazole on Days 1, 4 and 7
- 150 mg oteseconazole once daily for 7 days beginning on Day 14
- Oteseconazole 150 mg weekly for 11 weeks beginning on Day 28 post-initiation of treatment

L145: ## 6.2. Clinical Trials Intended to Demonstrate Efficacy
L146: ## 6.2.1. Trial CL-011
L147: ## 6.2.1.1. Design, Trial CL-011
L148: Trial CL-011 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter study evaluating the efficacy and safety of oteseconazole oral capsules for the prevention of VVC episodes in females with a history of RVVC. The design of the study followed advice provided by the Division during reviews of the protocol and supports the indication for reducing the incidence of recurrence of VVC. The full protocol synopsis is contained in Section 15 of this review.
L149: Subjects presenting with an acute VVC infection and a history of RVVC entered the induction phase and were treated with fluconazole. All subjects received three sequential 150 mg oral doses (every 72 hours) of fluconazole. Approximately 14 days after receipt of fluconazole, consented females were evaluated for resolution of their acute VVC episode. Females documented as having a signs and symptoms score of &lt;3 were eligible for the 12-week maintenance phase and were subsequently randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive either oteseconazole or matching placebo for 7 daily doses (1 week) of 150 mg, followed by 11 weekly doses of 150 mg, and then a 36-week follow-up period. The baseline/Day 1 visit was defined as the first day the study drug was received. Females whose acute VVC episodes were not resolved following fluconazole administration were considered screen failures.
L150: Study visits during treatment were scheduled as follows: a screening visit, Day -14 (\pm 2 days); a baseline/end-of-induction visit, on Day 1; a Day 14 (\pm 2 days) visit, a Week 6 visit (\pm 7 days); and an end-of-treatment visit Week 12 (\pm 14 days). After the week 12 visit, subjects were followed every 6 weeks until the end of study visit at Week 48 (\pm 14 days).
L151: Since the effect of oteseconazole for the treatment of an acute VVC episode was unknown, treatment of the acute VVC episode with fluconazole, a known effective treatment for VVC, was provided to all patients. Randomizing successfully treated patients following the induction phase allowed for a more controlled assessment of recurrence for oteseconazole versus placebo during the maintenance phase.
L152: ## 6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial CL-011
L153: Eligible subjects were generally healthy nonpregnant, nonlactating postmenarchal females \ge 12 years of age with a history of RVVC and an acute VVC infection at screening. A history of RVVC was defined as having had at least three episodes of acute VVC in the past 12 months, as evidenced by the following:
L154: - Having an acute VVC episode at the time of screening, with a documented positive KOH or Gram stain performed at the investigational site
- Having had two or more *additional* episodes of acute VVC in the prior 12 months, with *at least one* of the episodes documented by a positive confirmatory laboratory test, i.e., culture, polymerase chain reaction, Affirm test, KOH test, Gram stain, a documented Papanicolaou (Pap) test.
L155: The acute VVC infection at screening required a clinical diagnosis of symptomatic acute VVC (i.e., a total signs and symptom score \ge 3) as well as a positive KOH wet mount preparation (or Gram stain) from a vaginal smear that demonstrated filamentous hyphae/pseudohyphae and/or budding yeast cells.
L156: Eligible subjects were required to have a composite vulvovaginal signs and symptoms score of \ge 3 at screening and a composite score of &lt;3 at the baseline visit, demonstrating acceptable resolution of the presenting VVC episode. Vulvovaginal signs (edema, erythema, and excoriation) and symptoms (burning, itching, and irritation) were assessed by the investigator and subject, respectively. The six signs and symptoms were scored based on severity (absent = 0; mild = 1; moderate = 2; severe = 3) and the individual scores were summed to calculate the total composite vulvovaginal signs and symptoms score.
L157: Subjects were ineligible for study participation if, at screening or on Day 1, they were noted to have the following key exclusionary criteria:
L158: - Concomitant vulvovaginitis caused by other pathogens (e.g., bacterial vaginosis, *Trichomonas vaginalis*, *Chlamydia trachomatis*, or *Neisseria gonorrhoeae*)
- An active human papillomavirus infection, as evidenced by visible condylomas on vulvovaginal examination at screening and Day 1
- The presence or a history of another vaginal or vulvar condition(s) that in the investigator's opinion would have confounded the interpretation of clinical response
- Use of any systemic (e.g., oral or injectable) corticosteroid therapy during the study or within 30 days prior to screening or use of topical vulvar or vaginal steroids during the study or within 7 days prior to screening
- A laboratory abnormality that in the opinion of the investigator was likely to introduce additional risk to the subject or might interfere with data interpretation
L159: Females were also excluded from study participation if they:
L160: - Were found to be pregnant at screening
- Had active substance abuse
- Had uncontrolled diabetes mellitus
- Had either a history of or an active cervical/vaginal cancer
L161: ## 6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial CL-011
L162: The primary efficacy endpoint was the occurrence of one or more culture-verified acute VVC episodes during the maintenance phase (post randomization through Week 48) in the intent-to-treat (ITT) population which included all randomized subjects. A culture-verified acute VVC episode during the maintenance phase (considered a recurrent episode) was defined as a positive culture for *Candida* species associated with a clinical signs and symptoms score of \ge 3.
L163: Subjects who may have been treated with VVC medication during the maintenance phase were not automatically considered as having a recurrent episode. This is a concern as treatment with a VVC medication might indicate a VVC episode. This will be addressed in Section 6.3.1.
L164: Key secondary endpoints included the following:
L165: - Time to first recurrence of a culture-verified acute VVC episode with signs and symptoms of \ge 3 during the maintenance phase.
L166: - Proportion of subjects with at least one positive culture for *Candida* species during the maintenance phase. Note that this is the microbiologic portion of the primary endpoint only.
- Proportion of subjects with at least one culture-verified acute VVC episode with signs and symptoms of \ge 3 post randomization through Week 24; note that this is the primary endpoint assessed at an earlier time point.
- Changes from screening through Week 48 in the 36-item short form survey (SF-36) mental component score (MCS) and patient reported outcome survey total score.
L167: The primary method of analysis was a chi-square test for active treatment versus placebo based on the ITT population. Time to recurrence of a culture-verified acute VVC episode was estimated using the method of Kaplan-Meier. A chi-square test was used to compare the active treatment group to the placebo group for each key secondary endpoint that was a proportion. The changes from baseline value for the SF-36 MCS and SF-36 total score were analyzed using repeated measure analysis of covariance models with fixed effects for treatment group, time point, treatment by time point interaction and baseline value and with a random effect of subject. A compound symmetry covariance structure was used.
L168: The hierarchical/gate-keeping method was used to control for the type I error rate of 0.05 for multiplicity of the key secondary endpoints (order as stated above).
L169: For handling subjects with missing data, the primary analysis approach was to impute missing values using a multiple imputation approach. This was used for both intermittent missing data, i.e., when a subject had a missed visit or a missed assessment of signs and symptoms or missing culture results, or if a subject had terminal missing data, i.e., if a subject discontinued from the trial. The missing values were imputed using baseline information of region, treatment, baseline body mass index (BMI), baseline age, ethnicity and visit. Additional details of the multiple imputation methods are given in Section 15.
L170: Additionally, sensitivity analyses were conducted using different methods for handling missing data. One method treats subjects with missing data as having had a recurrence. The analysis of time to recurrence censors subjects who discontinue the study.
L171: Assuming a treatment difference in subjects with one or more culture-verified acute VVC episodes during the maintenance phase (post randomization through Week 48) of 35% between oteseconazole and placebo and assuming that 50% of subjects on placebo have a recurrence, a sample size of 68 active subjects and 34 placebo subjects was determined to provide at least 95% power to detect this difference. A total of 102 subjects was determined to provide more than 90% power to detect reasonable differences between treatment groups in most of the pre-specified key secondary endpoints. However, a sample size of 160 active subjects and 80 placebo subjects was needed to provide 80% power to detect a treatment difference of 3.9 between the oteseconazole treatment group and the placebo group in the change from screening through Week 48 in the SF-36 MCS with a standard deviation of 10 points. Approximately 300 subjects were anticipated to be necessary for randomization to arrive at approximately 240 evaluable subjects. Approximately 600 subjects would be screened to randomize approximately 300 subjects that would meet the inclusion/exclusion criteria.
L172: ## 6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, Trial CL-011
L173: ## Subject Disposition, Demographics and Baseline Characteristics
L174: The trial was conducted at 97 sites in North America, Japan, and Europe. The first patient was enrolled on August 20, 2018, and the study was completed was on October 21, 2020.
L175: All screened and randomized subjects are summarized below in Table 8. A total of 553 subjects were screened. Among these 553 subjects, 438 subjects entered the induction phase. Following the induction phase, 326 subjects were randomized, in a 2:1 ratio and then entered into the maintenance phase. Two hundred seventeen subjects were randomized to oteseconazole and 109 to placebo. All randomized subjects were treated and received the study medication to which they were randomized. Therefore, the safety population and the ITT population are the same.

L179: Subject disposition is summarized in Table 9. Of the 326 subjects in the ITT population, 35 (16.1%) of 217 oteseconazole subjects and 18 (16.5%) of 109 placebo subjects were discontinued from the study. The most common reasons for discontinuation were withdrawal by subject (8.3%) and lost to follow-up (3.7%). The mean number of days (SD) of maintenance treatment in the ITT population was 79 (18.4) in oteseconazole and 80 (17.0) in placebo. Treatment compliance was high in both treatment arms. Daily compliance (defined as the number of doses taken divided by 7) during the daily dosing phase had a mean (SD) of 100% (4.7) for oteseconazole and 99% (6.8) for placebo. Compliance for the weekly dosing phase (number of doses divided by 11) was 93% (21.9) and 94% (19.8) in oteseconazole and placebo, respectively. The amount of follow-up was comparable for the two treatment arms with a mean (SD) follow-up time for oteseconazole subjects of 308.6 days (81.6) and 315.0 (77.0) days for placebo.

L184: Subjects' demographic and baseline characteristics, which were similar between the arms, are summarized for the ITT population in **Table 10**. The mean age (SD) was 34 (10.2) years old. The majority of participants were between 25 to 34 years of age, 53.9% in the oteseconazole arm and 53.2% in the placebo arm. A single subject, randomized to the oteseconazole arm, was &lt;18 years old, and only two participants, both in the oteseconazole arm, were ≥65 years old. A majority of subjects were White (72%) and Not Hispanic or Latino (92%). Latino females, most of whom identified as White, comprised 7.7% of the total ITT population.
L185: Nearly equal percentages of subjects, 42.9% and 43.6%, across both treatment arms were from North America and Europe, respectively. The majority of North American subjects were from the United States, while most European subjects were from Eastern Europe. 13.5% of all subjects were from Japan.

L198: The use of any concomitant medication was 175 (81%) in oteseconazole and 85 (78%) in placebo subjects in the safety population. The most frequently used concomitant medications were paracetamol (10% in oteseconazole and 14% in placebo) and levonorgestrel (12% in oteseconazole and 9% in placebo). Note that fluconazole was not considered a concomitant medication in the study report since the use of fluconazole was allowed per the protocol for the treatment of an episode VVC during the study. However, fluconazole was used by 57 (26%) subjects who received oteseconazole and 54 (50%) subjects who received placebo.
L199: ## 6.2.1.4.1. Efficacy Results
L200: The proportion of subjects with \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episodes during the maintenance phase (post randomization through Week 48) in the ITT population was the primary endpoint, and the results are summarized in Table 12.
L201: The number of subjects with \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episodes through Week 48 was 14 subjects in oteseconazole and 45 subjects in placebo. A total of 53 subjects, 42 from oteseconazole and 11 from placebo, did not have complete follow-up. Results of analysis using two methods for handling this lack of complete follow-up are presented in Table 12. The primary method used multiple imputation, and a second method imputed all subjects with missing data as recurrences. Both methods indicated the proportion of subjects with \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episodes through Week 48 were statistically significantly lower in the oteseconazole arm than in the placebo arm (p&lt;0.001). The average percentage of subjects with \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episode through Week 48 was 6.7% in oteseconazole and 42.8% in placebo using the multiple imputation (MI) method. In the analysis in which missing data were counted as failures, the estimated percentages were 25.8% for oteseconazole and 51.4% for placebo.

L209: The results of time to first recurrence of culture-verified acute VVC infection are summarized in **Table 13** and presented in the Kaplan Meier plot in **Figure 2**. Subjects with incomplete follow-up were censored at their last follow-up time in this analysis. Both treatment groups' median time to first recurrence of a culture-verified acute VVC episode were not reached because less than 50% of subjects had recurrence events during the study. When considering the timing of the events, more recurrence events through Week 12 occurred in the placebo arm (26 (23.9%)) than in the oteseconazole arm (4 (1.8%)). After treatment through Week 48, 10 recurrence events (4.6%) occurred in the oteseconazole arm and 19 recurrence events (17.4%) occurred in the placebo arm.

L221: ## 6.2.2. Trial CL-012
L222: ## 6.2.2.1. Design, Trial CL-012
L223: Trial CL-012 was a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study evaluating the efficacy and safety of oteseconazole oral capsules in the treatment of subjects with RVVC. Its design was identical to CL-011. Refer to 6.2.1.1 for details. The full protocol synopsis is contained in Section 15.1 of this review.
L224: This study design supported the indication of reducing the incidence of recurrence of VVC.
L225: ## 6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial CL-012
L226: The inclusion and exclusion criteria were the same as study CL-011. Refer to Section 6.2.1.2.
L227: ## 6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial CL-012
L228: The statistical methods were the same as Study CL-011. Refer to 6.2.1.3.
L229: ## 6.2.2.4. Results of Analyses, Trial CL-012
L230: ## Subject Disposition, Demographics and Baseline Characteristics
L231: The study was conducted at 84 sites in North America (United States and Canada) and Europe (Belgium, Czech Republic, Hungary, Romania, and Ukraine). The first patient was enrolled on October 4, 2018, and the study was completed on October 13, 2020.
L232: A total of 534 subjects were screened and of those, 425 subjects were entered into the induction phase. Following the induction phase, 330 subjects were entered into the maintenance phase and
L233: were randomized in a 2:1 ratio, with 220 subjects into oteseconazole and 110 subjects into placebo. Three subjects in the oteseconazole arm were not treated. Site 31-240 was found to have good clinical practice violations and had randomized two subjects to each treatment group prior to being shut down by the Applicant. Subjects from this site were excluded from the ITT population but were included in the safety population. The safety population therefore included 327 subjects: 217 subjects in the oteseconazole arm and 110 subjects in the placebo arm. The ITT population included 326 subjects: 218 subjects in the oteseconazole arm and 108 subjects in the placebo arm. Subjects' screening and randomization are summarized in Table 14.
L234: **Table 14. Subjects' Screening and Randomization, Trial CL-012**

L236: Source: Reviewer conducted analysis using ADSL dataset
L237: Subject disposition is summarized in Table 15. Of the 326 subjects in the ITT population, 27 (12.3%) of 218 oteseconazole subjects and 11 (10%) of 108 placebo subjects were discontinued from the study. The most common reasons for discontinuation were withdrawal of consent (6.7%) and lost to follow-up (2.1%). The mean number of days (SD) of maintenance treatment in the ITT population was 80 (17.0) in the oteseconazole arm and 78 (20.5) in the placebo arm. Treatment compliance was high in both treatment arms. Daily compliance (defined as the number of doses taken divided by 7) during the daily dosing phase had a mean (SD) of 98.6 (11.7) for oteseconazole and 99.1% (8.3) for placebo. Compliance for the weekly dosing phase (number of doses divided by 11) was 91.7% (24.3) and 90.1% (26.1) for oteseconazole and placebo, respectively. The amount of follow-up was comparable for the two treatment arms with a mean follow-up time for oteseconazole subjects of 310 days and 303 days for placebo.

L254: ## 6.2.2.4.1. Efficacy Results
L255: The proportion of subjects with \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episode during the maintenance phase (post randomization through Week 48) in the ITT population was the primary endpoint and the results are summarized in Table 18.
L256: Eight subjects in the oteseconazole arm and 39 subjects in the placebo arm had \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episode through Week 48. A total of 51 subjects, 38 on oteseconazole and 13 on placebo had incomplete follow-up. Results of analyses using two methods for handling this lack of complete follow-up are presented in Table 18. The primary method used multiple imputation, and a second method imputed all subjects with missing data as recurrences. Both methods resulted in statistically significantly lower rates of recurrences in the oteseconazole arm
L257: than in the placebo arm (p&lt;0.001). The average percentage of subjects with \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episode through Week 48 was 3.9% in the oteseconazole arm and 39.4% in the placebo arm using the MI method. The analysis method in which missing data were imputed as failures led to 46 (21.1%) subjects in the oteseconazole arm and 52 (48.1%) in the placebo arm with least one culture-verified acute VVC episode.

L277: ## 6.2.3. Trial CL-017
L278: ## 6.2.3.1. Design, Trial CL-017
L279: Trial CL-017 was a phase 3 multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel group study conducted to evaluate the safety and efficacy of oteseconazole capsules versus fluconazole followed by placebo in the treatment and prevention of recurrence of acute, symptomatic VVC episodes in females with a history of RVVC. See Section 15 for the full protocol synopsis of this study.
L280: Trial CL-017 was divided into a 2-week induction phase, an 11-week maintenance phase, and a 37-week follow-up period. Unlike Trials CL-011 and CL-012, participants in Trial CL-017 were randomized at the start of the induction phase. Subjects were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to one of the following two treatment arms:
L281: - Oteseconazole arm:Induction phase: 600 mg oteseconazole (4x150 mg capsules) on Day 1 and 450 mg oteseconazole (3x150 mg capsules) on Day 2 and;
- Maintenance phase: 150 mg Oteseconazole weekly for 11 weeks starting on Day 14
- Fluconazole/Placebo arm:Induction phase: 3 sequential 150 mg oral doses (every 72 hours) of over-encapsulated fluconazole
- Maintenance phase: placebo weekly for 11 weeks
L282: Blinding was maintained with the use of a double-dummy design.
L283: Eligible subjects were randomized and entered the induction phase. During the induction phase, subjects presenting with an acute VVC infection were treated with either oteseconazole or fluconazole. Subjects returned approximately 14 days after the first dose of oteseconazole or fluconazole for evaluation. If the acute VVC infection had resolved (defined by a signs and symptoms score of &lt;3), they entered the maintenance phase to continue onto their assigned treatment of either oteseconazole or a matching placebo regimen. If the acute VVC infection had not resolved, the subject was considered an induction failure and was to be carried forward as a failure for the primary analysis. Each participant was anticipated to remain in the study for approximately 50 weeks.
L284: Similar to CL-011 and CL-012, CL-017 supports an indication for reducing the incidence of recurrent VVC.
L285: (b) (4)
L286: (b) (4)
L287: ## 6.2.3.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial CL-017
L288: Eligible subjects included generally healthy postmenarchal, nonpregnant females \ge 12 years of age with an acute VVC infection and a history of RVVC, defined as:
L289: - Three or more patient-reported and/or laboratory-confirmed episodes of acute VVC in the past 12 months including the episode confirmed at screening
- At least one episode (not including the current episode) documented by a positive culture, polymerase chain reaction, Affirm test, KOH test, or a documented Pap test in the prior 12 months revealing filamentous hyphae/pseudohyphae and/or budding yeast cells.
L290: Study exclusions were similar to Trials CL-011 and CL-012. Subjects were ineligible for study participation if, at screening or on Day 1, they were noted to have the following key exclusion criteria:
L291: - Concomitant vulvovaginitis caused by other pathogens (e.g., bacterial vaginosis, *Trichomonas vaginalis*, *Chlamydia trachomatis*, or *Neisseria gonorrhoeae*)
- An active human papillomavirus infection, as evidenced by visible condylomas on vulvovaginal examination at screening and Day 1
L292: - The presence or a history of another vaginal or vulvar condition(s) that in the investigator's opinion would have confounded the interpretation of clinical response
- Use of any systemic (e.g., oral or injectable) corticosteroid therapy during the study or within 30 days prior to screening or used topical vulvar or vaginal steroids during the study or within 7 days prior to screening
- A laboratory abnormality that in the opinion of the investigator was likely to introduce additional risk to the subject or might interfere with data interpretation.
L293: Females were also excluded from study participation if they:
L294: - Were found to be pregnant at screening
- Had active substance abuse
- Had uncontrolled diabetes mellitus
- Had a history of or an active cervical/vaginal cancer
L295: Females of child-bearing potential were instructed to use appropriate contraceptive method(s) throughout the study.
L296: ## 6.2.3.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial CL-017
L297: The primary efficacy outcome measure was the proportion of subjects with one or more culture-verified acute VVC episodes (postrandomization through Week 50) in the ITT population, which included the subjects who failed clearing their infection during the induction phase. An acute VVC episode (considered a recurrent episode) was defined as a positive culture for *Candida* species and a clinical signs and symptoms score of \ge 3.
L298: Note that the phrase “which included the subjects who failed clearing their infection during the induction phase” was interpreted by the Agency to mean that subjects who failed clearing their initial infection would be carried forward as failures for the primary endpoint. This was important because subjects were randomized at the time of the initial episode such that effect on that episode was directly related to the treatment effect. This was discussed with the Applicant at the design stage of the trial. Therefore, for clarity, we interpreted the primary endpoint to be defined as the occurrence of either a failure of the initial treatment of the presenting VVC episode during the induction phase or the development of one or more culture-verified acute VVC episodes during the maintenance phase (postrandomization through Week 50) in the ITT population.
L299: As with Trials CL-011 and -012, subjects who may have been treated with additional VVC medication during the study were not automatically considered as having a recurrent episode. This will be addressed in Section 6.3.1.
L300: Key secondary endpoints include the following:
L301: - Proportion of subjects with resolved acute VVC infections (clinical signs and symptoms score of &lt;3) at Day 14 following treatment with oteseconazole or fluconazole in the ITT population
- Proportion of subjects with at least one culture-verified acute VVC episode with signs and symptoms score of \ge 3 during the maintenance phase (post-Day 14 through Week 50) in the ITT population
- Time to first recurrence of a culture-verified acute VVC episode with signs and symptoms of \ge 3 during the maintenance phase in the ITT population
L302: - Proportion of subjects with at least one positive culture for *Candida* species during the maintenance phase in the ITT population
L303: The hierarchical/gatekeeping method was used to control for the type I error rate at 0.05 for multiplicity of secondary endpoints. The protocol specified that the first secondary endpoint would be tested using a noninferiority hypothesis rather than superiority.
L304: The primary method of analysis was a comparison of the proportions of subjects with either a failure of the initial treatment of the presenting VVC episode during the induction phase or the development of one or more culture-verified acute VVC episodes during the maintenance phase through Week 50 using a chi-square test for test arm versus the control based on the ITT population which included all randomized subjects.
L305: (b) (4)
L306: (b) (4) Note that the study report included an “induction modified intent-to-treat population” (induction modified intent-to-treat [mITT]) defined as all randomized subjects who had a positive central KOH test at screening and a positive culture at screening. This definition is more in line with the primary analysis population recommended in the FDA VVC guidance document (August 2019) and will be the population used in this review when discussing the Day 14 results.
L307: Time to recurrence of an acute VVC episode was estimated using the Kaplan-Meier method. Subjects who failed to clear their acute infection during the induction phase were excluded from these analyses. A chi-square test was used to compare the active treatment group to the placebo group for each key secondary endpoint that was a proportion.
L308: For handling subjects with missing data, the primary analysis approach imputed missing values using a multiple imputation approach. This was used for both intermittent missing data, i.e., when a subject had a missed visit or a missed assessment of signs and symptoms or missing culture results, or if a subject has terminal missing data, i.e., if a subject discontinues from the trial. The missing values were imputed using baseline information of region, treatment, baseline BMI, baseline age, ethnicity and visit. Additional details of the multiple imputation methods are the same at those used for trial CL-011.
L309: Additionally, sensitivity analyses were conducted using different methods for handling missing data. One method treats subjects with missing data as having had a recurrence. The analysis of time to recurrence censors subjects who discontinue the study.
L310: Assuming a treatment difference in subjects with one or more culture-verified acute VVC episodes during the maintenance phase (postrandomization through Week 50) of 35% between oteseconazole and placebo, a sample size of 82 active subjects and 41 placebo subjects provided at least 90% power with a 50% recurrence rate for fluconazole/placebo. For the first key secondary endpoint, the proportion of subjects with resolved acute VVC infections (clinical signs and symptoms score of &lt;3) at Day 14 following treatment with oteseconazole or fluconazole, a sample size of 120 subjects in the oteseconazole arm and 60 subjects in the fluconazole/placebo arm provided at least 88% power to detect noninferiority between the treatment groups with a
L311: noninferiority margin of 15% and a type 1 error rate of 0.05. Therefore, the study planned to enroll 180 total subjects, including 120 subjects in the oteseconazole arm and 60 subjects in the fluconazole/placebo arm.
L312: ## 6.2.3.4. Results of Analyses, Trial CL-017
L313: ## Subject Disposition, Demographics and Baseline Characteristics
L314: The study was conducted at 84 sites in the United States. The first patient was enrolled on October 14, 2018, and the study was completed on October 13, 2020.
L315: The subjects' screening and randomization information is summarized in Table 20. A total of 251 subjects were screened, and 219 subjects were entered into the induction phase and randomized with a 2:1 ratio, resulting in 147 oteseconazole subjects and 72 fluconazole/placebo subjects. All subjects were treated and received the treatment they were randomized to with the exception of one subject randomized to oteseconazole who was not treated.

L319: The subjects' disposition is summarized Table 21. The ITT population included 219 subjects: 147 subjects in the oteseconazole arm and 72 subjects in the fluconazole/placebo arm. The safety population, which included all randomized and treated subjects, included 218 subjects: 146 subjects in the oteseconazole arm and 72 subjects in the fluconazole/placebo arm. The induction mITT population included 81 (55.2%) oteseconazole subjects and 38 (52.8%) fluconazole/placebo subjects. Subjects excluded from the induction mITT population included 118 subjects (80 oteseconazole and 38 fluconazole/placebo) who did not have a positive culture at screening and 1 oteseconazole subject who did not have a positive KOH at screening.
L320: The subjects who completed the study included 112 (76.2%) oteseconazole subjects and 55 (76.4%) fluconazole/placebo subjects. The most common reasons for discontinuation were due to induction failure (14.2%) and lost to follow-up (5.0%). The mean overall follow-up time for oteseconazole was 293 days and 295 days for fluconazole/placebo.

L338: ## 6.2.3.4.1. Efficacy Results
L339: The proportion of subjects who failed induction therapy or who had \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episode during post randomization through Week 50 in the ITT population was the primary endpoint and the results are summarized in Table 24. Oteseconazole demonstrated superiority to fluconazole/placebo in the proportion of subjects who either failed induction therapy or who had \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episode through Week 50 (p&lt;0.001) regardless of the analysis method used. The average percentage of subjects with \ge 1 culture-verified acute VVC episode through Week 50 was 10.3% in the oteseconazole arm and 42.9% in the placebo arm using the MI method. In the analysis method where missing assessments were counted as failures, 44 (29.9%) oteseconazole subjects and 40 (55.6%) placebo subjects had an event. It should be noted that two subjects in each treatment arm who failed induction therapy also had positive cultures at Day 14. While these subjects met the definition of a culture-verified acute VVC episode, they were counted as failures of induction therapy rather than known recurrence in Table 24.

L354: As discussed above, the primary endpoint for assessing treatment of VVC recommended in the guidance would typically be based on clinical cure requiring the resolution of all signs and symptoms (i.e., signs and symptoms score = 0) and it would be conducted in an mITT population similar to how the induction mITT population is defined for this study. L361: The secondary endpoint results of time to first recurrence of a culture-verified acute VVC episode during the maintenance phase (post Day 14 through Week 50) are summarized in Table 27 and presented in a Kaplan-Meier plot in Figure 4. Actual known recurrence events occurred in 4 (2.7%) oteseconazole subjects and in 24 (33.3%) fluconazole/placebo subjects. Oteseconazole demonstrated superiority to fluconazole/placebo in the time to first recurrence of culture verified acute VVC infection based on the hazard ratio of 0.06 (p&lt;0.001). Both treatment groups' median time to first recurrence of a culture-verified acute VVC episode were not reached because less than 50% of subjects had a recurrence event during the study. Note that subjects who did not enter the maintenance phase, including those who did not have successful treatment of their initial VVC episode or had no follow-up in the maintenance phase, were excluded from this analysis.
L362: The recurrence events during the treatment post Day 14 through Week 12 included 2 (1.4%) subjects in the oteseconazole arm and 11 (15.3%) subjects in the fluconazole/placebo arm. After treatment completion through Week 50, 2 recurrence events (1.4%) occurred in the oteseconazole arm and 13 recurrence events (18.1%) occurred in the fluconazole/placebo arm. More recurrence events occurred in the fluconazole/placebo arm during the treatment and follow-up periods than in the oteseconazole arm.

L374: ## 6.3. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of Benefit
L375: ## 6.3.1. Assessment of the Use of VVC Medication on the Interpretation of the Efficacy of Oteseconazole
L376: ## Issue
L377: Assessment of the use of VVC medication on the interpretation of the efficacy of oteseconazole.
L378: ## Background
L379: The definition of a recurrent episode of VVC required both a signs and symptoms score \ge 3 as well as a positive culture for *Candida*. Per the protocols, investigators were allowed to offer VVC medication to a subject if they deemed there to be a clinical need when the subject had a signs and symptoms score \ge 3 and a positive KOH. Whether or not someone was treated for a suspected VVC infection did not enter into the primary endpoint definition, but the use of other VVC medication during maintenance could confound the assessment of recurrence. Therefore, the use of VVC medication was explored and a sensitivity analysis in which subjects who received any other VVC medications were also treated as failures was considered to see if the use of VVC medication overly affected the results. Additionally, recurrence was alternatively defined based on a positive KOH rather than a positive culture as used in the primary endpoint definition. Recurrence defined in this manner may be more relevant from a patient perspective since a positive KOH was a trigger for treatment of VVC.
L380: ## Assessment
L381: **Table 28** summarizes the number of subjects who received VVC medication for a known recurrence, those who did not have a recurrence but who had incomplete follow-up, and those who had complete follow-up and no recurrence for Trials CL-011 and CL-012.
L382: **Table 28. Culture-Verified Acute VVC Recurrence and Receipt of VVC Medication During Maintenance (ITT Population), Trials CL-011 and CL-012**

(N=217)	Placebo

L386: Additional analyses of the primary endpoint that consider use of VVC medication as part of the definition of recurrence supported the significance of oteseconazole compared to placebo in the reduction of recurrence. For Trial CL-011, using multiple imputation to address subjects with incomplete data gave rates of recurrences of 27.3% on oteseconazole and 50.8% on placebo (p&lt;0.001). Alternatively, treating recurrences, VVC medication use, and incomplete follow-up as failures led to 93 (42.9%) events for oteseconazole compared to 63 (57.8%) for placebo (p=0.01). For Trial CL-012, using multiple imputation to handle subjects with incomplete data gave rates of recurrences of 21.3% on oteseconazole and 49.7% on placebo (p &lt;0.001). Treating subjects who had missing data as having an event leads to 76 (34.9%) events on oteseconazole and 61 (56.5%) events on placebo (p&lt;0.001).
L387: Recurrence based on the alternative definition of signs and symptoms score \ge 3 and positive KOH is summarized in **Table 29** for Trials CL-011 and CL-012. Most of the use of VVC medication was captured using this definition of recurrence as few subjects without a recurrence received VVC medication. When treating incomplete follow-up as a failure, the rates of recurrence were 41.5% for oteseconazole compared to 55.0% for placebo (p=0.02) in Trial CL-011 and 33.9% for oteseconazole compared to 52.8% for placebo (p=0.001) in Trial CL-012.

L392: Additional important information to consider is the number of times a subject was treated with VVC treatments during the maintenance phase. L394: For Trial CL-012, the mean number of VVC treatments on oteseconazole was significantly lower (0.35) than that on placebo (1.0). Similar to Trial CL-011, more placebo subjects than oteseconazole subjects were treated at least once with a VVC medication during the maintenance phase, and most received one or two treatments with a VVC medication, although less for oteseconazole subjects compared to placebo subjects. One oteseconazole subject was treated 8 times whereas 7 times was the maximum number of times a placebo subject was treated.

L418: ## Conclusion
L419: Although there was a large number of subjects who received additional VVC medication in both arms within each study, the difference between treatments, though smaller than that observed for the primary endpoint, was still significantly different in favor of the oteseconazole arm when the use of VVC medication was included in the definition of recurrence or when the definition of recurrence was based on a positive KOH rather than a positive culture. From a patient perspective, the need for additional VVC treatment due to the presence of signs and symptoms may be more important than having a culture-verified episode of VVC when determining the effect of maintenance treatment. Therefore, it is recommended that, in addition to the protocol-specified primary endpoint, the results of the analyses that include the use of VVC medication in the definition of recurrence or the alternate definition of recurrence also be included in Section 14: Clinical Studies of the product labelling.
