L693: **Clinical Reviewer's Comment:** The clinical reviewer identified no increases in the QTcF interval to suggest the potential for oteseconazole induced QTcF interval prolongations of &gt;500 msec, particularly among participants with baseline QTcF intervals ≤ 450 msec. There were no participants with a baseline QTcF interval of &gt;480 msec or &gt;500 msec. No subjects developed QTcF interval increases of &gt;480 msec to ≤ 500 msec or QTcF interval increases of &gt;500 msec while on oteseconazole or placebo.
L694: In addition, the reviewer conducted an analysis of the absolute change in baseline and post-baseline QTcF intervals. The reviewer found that most participants had an absolute post-baseline QTcF interval change of &lt;30 msec when compared to their baseline QTcF value. Eighteen (3.1%) and 6 (2.1%) participants in the oteseconazole and placebo arms, respectively, developed an absolute post-baseline change of ≥30 msec to &lt;60 msec from their baseline QTcF interval. Importantly, there were no subjects observed to have an absolute post-baseline QTcF interval increase of ≥60 msec. It is noted that there was no comparator positive control drug. Since the placebo comparator drug was not a positive control, no definitive statement could be
L695: *made on whether oteseconazole was not associated with QTcF interval prolongations based on the phase 3 trials. Nevertheless, in the context of the phase 3 trials, no QTcF prolongations were observed.*
L696: *The clinical reviewer performed CMQs and SMQs to search for any PTs suggestive of QTc prolongations (e.g., ventricular arrhythmias, torsades de pointes, cardiac arrest, sudden death) in the phase 3 (Trials CL-011, CL-012, and CL-017) and the phase 2b (Trial CL-006) trials and found none. The search only yielded such PTs as: atrial fibrillation (1 subject), bradycardia, palpitation, syncope (1 subject), and tachycardia among participants in the oteseconazole arm. There were no noted differences between the oteseconazole and placebo arms.*
L697: ## **Drug Allergy/Hypersensitivity/Anaphylactic Reactions**
L698: The reviewer identified several PTs potentially suggestive of hypersensitivity reactions in the phase 3 integrated summary of safety (ISS) database. These PTs included: blister (reported term: vesicles on trunk), dermatitis; dermatitis allergic; dermatitis contact; drug eruption; drug hypersensitivity; mouth ulceration; rash generalized; swelling; swelling face; and urticaria. There was a total of 27 TEAEs, none of which were SAEs, that were reported in a total of 21 subjects: 15 (2.6%; 15/580) subjects in the oteseconazole arm (with 21 TEAEs) and 6 (2.1%; 6/291) in placebo subjects (6 TEAEs). A single case of dermatitis occurred pre-treatment in a subject in the oteseconazole arm. No cases of anaphylaxis were reported among oteseconazole-exposed subjects. Narratives for these subjects can be found in Section 17.3.3 of the Appendix.
L699: ## **Other Adverse Events of Special Interest**
L700: Outside of the above-described adverse events of special interest (AESI), the clinical reviewer performed Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities -based Adverse Event Diagnostics CMQ and SMQ searches of the phase 3 trials (Trials CL-011, CL-012, and CL-017) and of the supportive phase 2b trial (Trial CL-006) to identify several AESI that have been associated with at least one or more azole-class drugs including: (a) adrenal dysfunction, (b) pancreatitis, (c) phototoxicity, (d) renal impairment, (e) seizure, and (f) visual disturbances.
L701: Neither the CMQs nor SMQs yielded any safety signals for potential cases of adrenal dysfunction, anaphylaxis, renal impairment, phototoxicity, seizures, and/or visual disturbances. A single subject in the oteseconazole arm of Trial CL-006, Subject (b) (6) had two treatment-emergent SAEs, one of which was an SAE resulting in withdrawal of oteseconazole and coded to the PT pancreatitis (Study Days 25 – 33). The event was deemed unrelated to oteseconazole exposure and occurred in a participant with a known history of chronic pancreatitis. This subject was discussed in greater detail under Section 7.6.7.1 Serious Adverse Events for Trial CL-006, and the narrative can be found in Section 17.3 of the Appendix.
L702: ## 7.6.7.2. Summary of Safety Findings From Other Studies
L703: ## Overall Adverse Event Summary, Nonpivotal Phase 1 and 2 Trials (Trial CL-004 and Trial CL-005)
L704: ## Phase 2a Study CL-004 (Acute VVC)
L705: The Applicant conducted a small phase 2a multi-center, randomized, double-blind, active-controlled, parallel-group proof of concept trial evaluating three separate oteseconazole dosing regimens: 300 mg daily x 3 days (900 mg total); 600 mg daily x 3 days (1800 mg total); and 600 mg twice daily x 3 days (3600 mg) all compared to single-dose fluconazole in the treatment of patients with moderate to severe acute VVC. The safety population was comprised of 55 subjects, each randomized to one treatment arm: a total of 14 subjects were randomized to the 900 mg arm (300 mg daily); 12 subjects were randomized to the 1800 mg arm (600 mg daily); 14 subjects were randomized to the 3600 mg arm (600 mg twice daily); and 15 were randomized to the active control single-dose fluconazole.
L706: A total of 62 treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) occurred among 55 subjects, with the fluconazole arm having the greatest number of TEAEs (22), followed by the oteseconazole 900 mg group (19 TEAEs); the 1800 mg group (17 TEAEs) and the 3600 mg group (4 TEAEs). There were no treatment-emergent SAEs that occurred in any treatment arm during this trial. There were no subjects with CPK elevations in the oteseconazole arm.
L707: The most common TEAEs across all oteseconazole arms were: PT: nasopharyngitis (5 TEAEs, 5 subjects) (pooled PT: upper respiratory tract infection, nasopharyngitis, oropharyngeal pain [reported term: sore throat] and pharyngitis); followed by bacterial vaginosis (3 TEAEs, 3 subjects) and nausea (3 TEAEs, in 2 subjects). The majority of TEAEs in the placebo arm were reported under the Infections and infestations SOC (11 TEAEs, 6 subjects), with the most commonly cited TEAEs being: bacterial vaginosis (2 TEAEs, 2 subjects), nasopharyngitis (2 TEAEs, 2 subjects), and Pregnancy (2 TEAEs, 2 subjects) under the Pregnancy, puerperium, and perinatal conditions SOC.
L708: Most TEAEs in the oteseconazole 900 mg arm occurred under the Infections and infestations SOC (9 TEAEs), followed by the Skin and subcutaneous tissue disorders SOC (3 TEAEs). BV and nausea were the most commonly reported TEAEs (2 TEAEs); however, the latter PT occurred in the same subject and was deemed to be related to the study drug by the Investigator. The 2 cases of BV were not deemed to be related to oteseconazole. AESIs included the PTs dermatitis contact (Study Day 8-29) which was deemed unrelated to oteseconazole by the Study Investigator and the PT urticaria (2 TEAEs) which occurred in the same patient (Study Days 8-35 and 35-41), with the first occurrence deemed related to oteseconazole. The Study Investigator assessed a single TEAE coded to the PT dyspareunia (Study Days 26-30) as being related to the drug. All but one TEAE resolved, many required new therapeutic treatments, and none had a severity intensity of greater than 2.
L709: The majority of TEAEs in the oteseconazole 1800 mg cohort occurred in the Nervous system disorder SOC (5 TEAEs), followed by the Gastrointestinal disorders SOC and the Infections and infestations arm (3 TEAEs each) SOC. Nausea (3 events) and dizziness (2 events) were the most frequently reported TEAEs; however, four of these five events occurred in the same subjects. All were self-limited and were believed to be related to the drug. Otherwise, there was one case each
L710: of bacterial vaginosis; headache, dysgeusia, and vulvovaginal pruritus that were deemed by the Study Investigator to be related to the study drug. In addition, and based on the presumed oteseconazole safety profile, there was a single TEAE of UTI that was deemed to be unrelated to oteseconazole exposure. Notably, there was a single subject with a PT coded to “Liver function test increased” (Study Day 18 to 32) that was deemed as unrelated to study drug. Most all events were self-limited. Several subjects, including those with BV and UTIs, required a new treatment.
L711: As stated earlier, there were a total of 4 TEAEs reported in the 3600 mg oteseconazole arm: two occurring in the same subject under the Reproductive system and breast disorders SOC (PTs: vulvovaginal pruritus and vulvovaginal erythema), and one each occurring in the Infections and infestations SOC (PT: pharyngitis) and Gastrointestinal disorders SOC (PT: abdominal pain). None of the reported events were assessed as being related to the study arm and three were reported to have resolved.
L712: ## **Phase 2b Trial CL-005 (Onychomycosis)**
L713: The Applicant conducted a 259 participant (ITT Population) dose ranging trial evaluating oteseconazole safety, tolerability, efficacy, and PK study in males and females with onychomycosis. Trial CL-005 was a multi-center, randomized, double-blind, active-controlled, parallel-group trial. The safety findings in Trial CL-005 were similar to the safety findings in the oteseconazole arms in the phase 3 trials and the supportive phase 2 trial CL-006, namely nasopharyngitis; sinusitis, and nausea.
L714: The Safety Population consisted of 212 persons randomized into four oteseconazole treatment arms: 53 subjects were randomized into the oteseconazole 4200 LD/300 mg 10 weeks arm; 53 subjects were randomized into the oteseconazole 4200 LD/300 mg 22 weeks arm; 52 subjects were randomized into the oteseconazole 8400 LD/600 mg 10 weeks arm; 54 subjects were randomized into the oteseconazole 8400 LD/600 mg 22 weeks arm, and 47 subjects were randomized into the placebo arm. Overall, there were a total of 471 adverse events (AEs) occurring in: 122 of 212 (57.6%) oteseconazole subjects (across all oteseconazole arms) with 395 TEAEs and 27 of 47 subjects (57.5%) in the placebo arm who experienced a total of 76 TEAEs.
L715: Across all oteseconazole treatment arms, the majority of all TEAEs occurred in the Infections and infestations SOC (76 TEAEs); followed by the Gastrointestinal disorders SOC (57 TEAEs); the Injury, poisoning and procedural complications SOC (50 TEAEs); Musculoskeletal and connective tissue disorders SOC (42 TEAEs); and the Skin and subcutaneous tissue disorders SOC (36 TEAEs). There was a total of 14 SAEs that occurred in 9 subjects across all oteseconazole treatment arms, including three subjects with multiple SAEs (Subject [REDACTED](b)(6) PT: osteoarthritis x 2; Subject [REDACTED](b)(6) PT: hemorrhagic anemia, acute kidney injury, pyrexia, multiple fractures; and Subject [REDACTED](b)(6) PTs: pulmonary embolism and artery dissection). The most frequent TEAEs occurring across oteseconazole treatment arms were: nasopharyngitis (15.1%) (32 of 212 subjects) (pooled PTs: upper respiratory tract infection [18 subjects]; nasopharyngitis [13 subjects]; oropharyngeal pain [1 subject]; and pharyngitis streptococcal [1 subject]); ingrowing nail (6.1%) (13 of 212 subjects); sinusitis (5.2%) (11 of 212 subjects) (pooled PTs: sinusitis [11 subjects], acute sinusitis [1 subject]) and nausea (4.7%) (10 of 212 subjects).
L716: There was a single subject, (Subject CL-005 (b) (6)), in the oteseconazole 4200 mg/300 mg x 10 weeks arm with a reported, self-limited Grade 1, on-treatment, non-SAE, TEAE with a PT coded to “Blood creatine phosphokinase increased” (Study Days 29 to 35). The final day of oteseconazole exposure in this subject was Study Day 85. The event reportedly required no change in oteseconazole dosing and was determined by the Study Investigator to be unrelated to oteseconazole.
L717: Allergic/hypersensitivity reactions, an AESI, were reported across all four oteseconazole arms (PTs: dermatitis [2 TEAEs, in 2 subjects]), dermatitis allergic, dermatitis atopic, dermatitis contact (5 TEAEs, in 2 subjects), and hypersensitivity (reported term: allergic reaction, occurred post oteseconazole treatment, Study Days 318-329).
L718: The SOC with the most TEAEs in the placebo arm was the Infections and infestations SOC (21 TEAEs), followed by the Gastrointestinal disorders SOC (12 TEAEs), and the Musculoskeletal and connective tissue disorders SOC (6 TEAEs). The most common TEAE in the placebo arm was nasopharyngitis (pooled PTs: upper respiratory tract infection and nasopharyngitis) (13 TEAEs, 7 subjects).
L719: ## Phase 1 Studies
L720: The Applicant conducted a total of eight phase 1 trials in healthy, adult subjects, including five trials that were included in the Applicant’s ISS data set (201 subjects in ISS: 177 in the oteseconazole arm and 24 in the placebo arm). These trials were:
L721: - CL-001, a first-in-human single dose escalation trial; (n=23 subjects, 33 AEs)
- CL-002, a multiple dose escalation; (n=12 subjects, 20 AEs)
- CL-008, an ethno bridging trial; (n=17 subjects, 35 AEs)
- CL-013, a food effect trial; (n=17 subjects, 45 AEs)
- CL-016, an absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion trial; (n=9 subjects, 37 AEs)
L722: Of the five phase 1 trials included in the Applicant’s ISS data set, 78 subjects had a total of 170 AEs. Seventy-six of these subjects had the 162 observed TEAEs discussed below: 66 in the oteseconazole arm (with a total of 147 TEAEs) and 10 subjects in the placebo arm (with a total of 15 TEAEs). The oteseconazole doses studied in these Phase 1 studies were: 5 mg (4 subjects); 10 mg (3 subjects); 20 mg (3 subjects); 40 mg (5 subjects); 80 mg (4 subjects); 150 mg (16 subjects); 160 mg (1 subject); 300 mg (6 subjects); 320 mg (5 subjects) and 600 mg (19 subjects).
L723: The SOCs with 10 or more reported TEAEs in the oteseconazole arm, in descending order were: the Nervous system disorders SOC (35 TEAEs); the Gastrointestinal disorders SOC (27 TEAEs); the Musculoskeletal and connective tissue disorders SOC (23 TEAEs); the General disorders and administration site conditions SOC (18 TEAEs); and the Injury, poisoning and procedural complications and Skin and subcutaneous tissue disorders SOCs (10 TEAEs each). The most commonly occurring TEAEs among oteseconazole participants were reported under the following PTs: headache (25 TEAEs; in 17 participants); nausea (8 TEAEs; in 8 participants); constipation (7 TEAEs; in 6 participants); back pain and myalgia (6 TEAEs; in 6 participants).
L724: Notably, 4 subjects in the oteseconazole phase 1 trials had TEAEs coded to the PT: blood creatine phosphokinase elevated. These occurred in four subjects receiving four different oteseconazole doses: 5-mg (Study Days 4-16), 10-mg (Study Days 24-73), 40-mg (Study Day 35
L725: to 41), and 320-mg (Study Days 49 to 51). The subjects who received oteseconazole 5 mg and 10-mg were in the single dose study and the subjects who received 40 mg and 320-mg doses were in the 7-day multiple dose escalation study. The reported CPK elevations had an onset of Study Day 4 through Study Day 49 relative to the start of oteseconazole exposure treatment. Three of the four participants were reported to have recovered from the event, with a single event with an outcome reported as unknown. None of these reported events were SAEs and none were deemed to be related to the study drug by the Study Investigator. Actions take with study drug were reported as unknown for the two subjects in the multiple ascending dose study and not applicable for the two subjects in the single dose study.
L726: There were six subjects in the oteseconazole arm who were reported as having myalgia, one of whom, Subject (b)(6) (PT: myalgia, Study Days 7-11), was also reported to have had elevated CPK levels (Study Day 4-16). A single subject in the oteseconazole arm (Subject (b)(6) 10-mg) was reported to have had a TEAE coded to ALT increased (Study Day 7-14), which was self-limited and deemed by the Study Investigator to be related to the study drug. This subject was also observed to have CPK elevations (Study Days 24-73), although these events did not occur concurrently and therefore were likely unrelated. There were no PTs suggestive of QT prolongation.
L727: There was a total of 15 TEAEs that occurred among 10 subjects in the phase 1 study placebo arm. The SOCs with the most TEAEs in this arm, were, in descending order: the Musculoskeletal and connective tissue disorders SOC (4 TEAEs); the Investigations SOC (3TEAEs); and the Nervous system disorders SOC and Skin and subcutaneous tissue disorders SOC (with 2 TEAEs each). The most common TEAE in the placebo arm was the PT: back pain (2 subjects), with all other PTs reported in one subject each. Notable TEAEs in the placebo arm were coded to the PTs: arthralgia, ALT increased, and blood creatine phosphokinase (with each of these PTs occurring in one subject).
L728: ## 7.6.7.3. 120 Day Safety Update
L729: In their 120-Day Safety Report, the Applicant provided pregnancy updates from completed phase 3 Trials CL-012 and CL-017; as well as safety data from their completed thorough QT (TQT) study (Trial CL-018); their ongoing hepatic and renal studies (Trial CL-019 and CL-020, respectively); and additional safety data on several ongoing studies conducted outside of the United States. According to the Applicant no new oteseconazole safety signals were identified based on the reported data.
L730: ## Phase 1 Thorough QT (TQT) Study (CL-018)
L731: The Applicant reported that there were no clinically relevant effects on heart rate (HR) or cardiac conduction (the PR and QRS intervals) among the 29 oteseconazole exposed subjects (oteseconazole 600 mg daily administered on Days 1 to 13, and a single dose of 1200 mg administered on Day 14). Of the 141 TEAEs that occurred in the study, none were determined to be severe. The most frequent (\ge 7\%) on-treatment TEAEs (Days 1 to 13) were headache, fatigue, nausea, and constipation; and the most frequent post-treatment TEAEs (Days 14+) were headache, dizziness, nausea, and constipation. There were no reported deaths or SAEs.
L732: ## **Hepatic and Renal Impairment**
L733: No deaths, SAEs, discontinuations, or any significant findings were reported in the ongoing hepatic (Trial CL-019) or renal (Trial CL-020) impairment studies.
L734: ## **Pregnancy Updates**
L735: There were three pregnancies updated, two from Trial CL-012 and one from Trial CL-017, that included two live, healthy births (including one premature birth at 32-weeks), and one elective abortion.
L736: ## **Ongoing Clinical Studies**
L737: The Applicant reported that the three studies conducted in support of the foreign marketing applications yielded no new oteseconazole-related safety signals. According to the Applicant, there were no reported deaths, nonfatal SAEs, TEAEs resulting in discontinuations, or any other reported significant findings.
L738: For further details on the Applicant's thorough QT (TQT) study (Trial CL-018) and hepatic and renal studies (Trials CL-019 and CL-020, respectively), please refer to Section 8.1 of this review.
L739: ## **7.7. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of Risk**
L740: ## **7.7.1. Nonclinical Ocular Findings and Implications for Labeling**
L741: ## **Issue**
L742: Ocular abnormalities were observed in rat pups born to dams dosed with oteseconazole while pregnant in four separate pre- and postnatal toxicity studies. Ocular abnormalities included cataracts/opacities, sometimes accompanied by exophthalmos, bupthalmos, optic nerve/retinal atrophy, lens degeneration and hemorrhage and were observed in the offspring of rats administered oteseconazole from Gestation Day 6 through Lactation Day 20 at 7.5 mg/kg day, (about 3.5 times the recommended human dose based on area under the concentration-time curve [AUC] comparisons). Details of these studies are included in Section III: Appendix Pharmacology Toxicology: Additional Information and Assessment.
L743: ## **Background**
L744: The Applicant is proposing to market VIVJOA to reduce the incidence of recurrent vulvovaginal candidiasis, a condition common among females of reproductive potential. These ocular findings may indicate a risk of cataracts, opacities and other ocular findings in the children of patients who are pregnant when the drug is present in the plasma. The prolonged drug exposure window of approximately 690 days (based on 5 times the half-life) of oteseconazole makes it very difficult to adequately mitigate these potential risks.
L745: ## Assessment
L746: Table 53 shows the increased incidences of ocular abnormalities in Study 1234003 in rat pups born to mothers dosed with oteseconazole via oral gavage once daily from Gestation Day 6 through Lactation Day 20. Similar increases in ocular abnormalities were also observed in Studies 1234001, 1234002 and 1234004.
L747: **Table 53. Number of Animals With Specific Ophthalmology Findings on Postnatal Day 21 in Rat Pups Born to Mothers Dosed With Oteseconazole via Oral Gavage Once Daily From Gestation Day 6 Through Lactation Day 20**
L748: Findings	0 mg/kg
N=144	7.5 mg/kg
N=132
Cataract	6	31
Hyphema	1	19
Corneal ulceration	0	4
Corneal perforation	1	2
Bupthalmos	0	3
Iridial hemorrhage	0	1
L749: Source: Reviewer generated data based on data from Study #1234003
L750: These findings will be described in the physician's prescribing information.
L751: ## Conclusion
L752: Based on findings of ocular abnormalities in several pre-clinical studies, coupled with oteseconazole's prolonged drug exposure window of approximately 690 days in humans (which would preclude reasonable use of birth control), the review team concluded that the administration of oteseconazole in females of childbearing potential should be contraindicated.
L753: ## 7.7.2. CPK Elevations
L754: ## Issue
L755: - Transient, largely asymptomatic, unexplained elevations in CPK of up to \ge 10 \times ULN (Grade 3) and \ge 20 \times ULN (Grade 4) were observed without evidence of acute kidney injury. The DAIDS toxicity grading scale was used to evaluate CPK elevations. See also Section 7.6.6., Laboratory Findings, and Table 44.
- Most CPK elevations were accompanied by mild elevations of AST and/or ALT.
- Subject narratives describing each event are found in Section 17.4 of the review.
L756: ## Background
L757: When using the DAIDS toxicity grading scale, an imbalance in the frequency of Grade 3 and Grade 4 CPK elevations was detected between the oteseconazole and placebo arms in the combined phase 3 trials (11 of 569; 1.9% versus 2 of 275, 0.7%) and in the supportive phase 2b trial (Trial CL-006) (6 of 160; 3.8% versus 0 of 45; 0.0%). It is noted that a 2:1 randomization ratio was used in the phase 3 trials (CL-011, CL-012, and CL-017); whereas a 1:1:1:1:1 randomization scheme was used for the phase 2b trial (4 dosing cohorts received oteseconazole and a single cohort received placebo).
L758: ## Assessment
L759: Using the DAIDS toxicity grading scale, CPK elevations as high as \ge 20 \times \text{ULN} were observed either during treatment with oteseconazole, within one-month post-oteseconazole exposure, between 2-4 months postexposure, and in some instances 5-8 months post-oteseconazole exposure. When observing subject level CPK data it is challenging to clearly implicate oteseconazole as being a contributor to this safety finding; however, when looking at the data in aggregate it appeared that elevations out to 4 months post treatment were not unlikely given oteseconazole's half-life of 114-138 days (3.8 months). In most cases, the observed CPK elevations co-occurred with Grade 1 to 4 AST elevations, which varied based on the amplitude of the CPK elevation. ALT elevations were also observed with increased CPK levels, but increases in ALT levels did not occur as consistently with CPK elevations as increases in AST. This is most likely because AST is found in skeletal muscle whereas ALT is more specific to the liver. Even in the setting of Grade 4 CPK elevations, there was no evidence of resultant acute kidney injury associated with the CPK elevations.
L760: An information request (IR) was sent to the Applicant on September 24, 2021, requesting any collateral information, including whether affected participants engaged in strenuous exercise, drug and alcohol use (exclusions), or exhibited baseline electrolytes disorders (e.g., hypokalemia, hypophosphatemia, or hypocalcemia) that could explain these elevations. The reviewer could not identify any patients with the observed elevations who were taking lipid lowering medications such as statins or fibrates.
L761: In most instances, the Applicant did not consider these elevations to be causally linked to oteseconazole administration given the transience and temporality of the findings. However, they were unable to provide alternate explanations for the observed findings.
L762: It should be noted that most CPK elevations in the subject narratives discussed in Section 17.4 of the Appendix were not identified by either the Study Investigator or the Applicant as TEAEs, but rather were independently identified by the clinical reviewer in search of post-baseline maximal CPK values that were at or around \ge 1000 \text{ U/L}. CPK elevations have been documented, in the postmarket setting, in other azole-class drugs, including: itraconazole which has been associated with “blood creatine phosphokinase increased.” In addition, “creatine phosphokinase increased” has been identified as a less common adverse reaction associated with voriconazole (under the Metabolic and Nutrition SOC).
L763: In a December 7, 2021, reply to a clinical and clinical pharmacology IR, the Applicant attributed the observed CPK imbalances to the differences in randomization scheme employed in the clinical trials. The Applicant indicated that risk factors most frequently associated with CPK elevations (e.g., trauma, seizure, medications, limb compression, electrolytes disorders, and viral infections) had not been identified in most subjects with these elevations. However, the Applicant acknowledged that for all subjects with witnessed CPK elevations, they could not confirm whether subjects engaged in strenuous exercise or substance abuse (e.g., alcohol, cocaine, methamphetamine). It is noted, however, that alcohol and drug use were exclusions for study participation.
L764: As part of the IR, the Applicant conducted an exposure-response analysis in order to investigate the potential association between oteseconazole exposure and the occurrence of a Grade 3/4 CPK elevation, or between oteseconazole exposure and the time to first occurrence of a Grade 3/4 CPK elevation. The Applicant's analysis used quartiles of maximum plasma concentration (C_{\text{max}})
L765: at the time of a Grade 3/4 CPK elevation or at any time for subjects without CPK elevation as an exposure metric for oteseconazole, and included the placebo/fluconazole arm as a comparator bin (null exposure). The Applicant's analysis did not identify a positive relationship either between oteseconazole concentrations and Grade 3/4 CPK elevations or between oteseconazole concentration and the time to first occurrence of the Grade 3/4 CPK elevation. The reviewer's evaluation used different quantiles of either C_{max} or average oteseconazole concentrations (C_{avg}), as oteseconazole exposure metrics. The reviewer did not identify a consistent trend or a statistically significant relationship either between the oteseconazole exposure and the occurrence of Grade 3/4 CPK elevations or between the oteseconazole exposures in patients with CPK elevation and the time to first occurrence of CPK elevation. In conclusion, under the studied and the proposed dosage for RVVC, there is no clear evidence of an association between the extent of oteseconazole exposure and the occurrence of CPK elevations (most of which were transient in nature).
L766: ## Conclusion
L767: The observed CPK elevations will be described under sub-section 6.1 Clinical Trial Experience of the VIVJOA label. CPK elevations have also been noted in the US prescribing information for the azole antifungals itraconazole and voriconazole, but not in the fluconazole or posaconazole US prescribing information. With the exception of one subject, Subject (b)(6) in Trial CL-017 who developed a Grade 4 CPK elevation of 14833 U/L (77.3 x ULN) and a concomitant TEAE of myalgia, most subjects with CPK elevations were otherwise asymptomatic.
L768: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization Therapeutic Individualization
L769: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L770: ## Hepatic Impairment
L771: The effect of hepatic impairment on the PK of oteseconazole was not studied. In vitro, oteseconazole was not metabolized in incubations with pooled human liver microsomes, and no metabolites were identified in the human mass balance study. Results of the mass balance study showed that out of 82% of administered radioactivity that was recovered, 56% was in feces. Because of the prolonged fecal recovery of radiolabeled oteseconazole, biliary excretion appears to be the primary elimination pathway for oteseconazole (accounting for the excretion of an estimated 66% of the absorbed dose); therefore, hepatic impairment may impact the elimination of oteseconazole. Based on the lack of significant hepatic metabolism, and the acceptable safety profile of oteseconazole in patients with RVVC at 2-fold higher doses (see Section 7.6.1) than the recommended dose, mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A) is not expected to have a clinically meaningful impact on the PK of oteseconazole. However, there is insufficient information to predict the effect of moderate or severe hepatic impairment on the PK of oteseconazole. Therefore, in patients with moderate to severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B-C), use of oteseconazole is not recommended due to the possible risk of significant drug accumulation.
L772: ## Renal Impairment
L773: A dedicated renal impairment study was not conducted. Results of the human mass balance study showed that out of 82% of administered radioactivity that was recovered, 26% was in urine. In population PK analysis, mild to moderate renal impairment (based on estimated glomerular filtration rate calculated using the modification of diet in renal disease [MDRD] equation) was not identified as a significant covariate for oteseconazole clearance as shown in Figure 6. Based on this analysis, the proposed dose is expected to be safe in patients with mild to moderate renal impairment. Population PK analysis of the impact of severe renal impairment on CL was less informative because only 2 subjects were identified under this renal function category. Furthermore, oteseconazole has not been studied in subjects with ESRD. Because experience is limited in these populations, use of oteseconazole is not recommended in patients with severe renal impairment or ESRD.
L774: Figure 6. Analysis of Effect of Renal Function on Oteseconazole Clearance
L775: Source: Reviewer analysis
Abbreviations: CKD, chronic kidney disease.
L776: ## Other Intrinsic Factors
L777: Population PK analysis indicated that sex, race and age (within adults) do not have a clinically significant effect on the PK of oteseconazole. In subjects weighing &lt;40 kg, population PK
L778: predicted AUC is up to 2-fold higher than the AUC in subjects with body weight 60 – 70 kg (See Section 14.3). Because only 1 subject weighing &lt;40 kg (with actual weight 36 kg) was identified across phase 3 clinical studies, the clinical significance of this observation is unknown. Notably, subjects enrolled in phase 3 trials weighing 40 to 59 kg did not appear to have increased TEAEs compared to those weighing at least 60 kg. Therefore, based on the available information, no dosage adjustment is needed based on body weight.
L779: ## 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L780: ## 8.2.1. Food Effect
L781: The review team recommends that oteseconazole be administered with food based on the following available information:
L782: - The dedicated food effect study (Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-013) evaluated the impact of high-fat, high-calorie (900-1000 kcal with 500-600 kcal from fat) and low-fat, low-calorie (650 kcal with 117 kcal from fat) meals on the PK of oteseconazole following administration of the to-be-marketed capsule formulation. Oteseconazole AUC increased by 36% with a high-fat meal but was unimpacted by a low-fat meal. Given the lack of an Exposure-Response for oteseconazole, the clinical relevance of the 36% increase in exposure with high fat, high calorie food cannot be ascertained. Nonetheless, administering oteseconazole with food (particularly, high fat food) could confer therapeutic benefit.
- In the food effect study, the incidence of AEs was higher in the fasted state and with a low-fat meal, (i.e., 50%, N=12/cohort) compared to administration of oteseconazole with a high-fat meal (i.e., 17%, N=12).
- In Phase 3 trials, patients were instructed to take oteseconazole with their largest meal of the day. Patients in the Phase 2 RVVC trial were simply instructed to take oteseconazole with food. Although neither the caloric contents nor types of food consumed were recorded for these trials, ultimately, the efficacy of oteseconazole in these studies was evaluated under fed conditions.
L783: ## 8.2.2. Drug Interactions
L784: It is unlikely that oteseconazole PK is affected by concomitant medications because oteseconazole does not undergo metabolism and is not a substrate of major transporters, P-glycoprotein (P-gp), breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), organic anion transporting polypeptides (OATP)1B1, OATP1B3, organic cation transporter (OCT)2, OAT1, and OAT3.
L785: It is unlikely that oteseconazole would impact the PK of the concomitant medications, except for BCRP substrates. The review team recommended that “Concomitant use of oteseconazole may increase the exposure of drugs that are BCRP substrates (e.g., rosuvastatin), which may increase the risk of adverse reactions associated with these drugs. Use the lowest possible starting dose or consider reducing the dose of these drugs and monitor for adverse reactions.”
L786: The Applicant did not conduct in vitro studies to evaluate whether oteseconazole is a substrate or an inhibitor of the multidrug and toxic compound extrusion (MATE) transporters which are expressed on the apical side of the kidney tubules (i.e., MATE1 and MATE2-K) and bile canaliculi (i.e., MATE1), where they mediate renal tubular secretion and biliary secretion,
L787: respectively. The Applicant asserted that based on overlapping substrate specificity between OCTs and MATE, and oteseconazole's physicochemical properties (i.e., high molecular weight, neutral charge, high protein binding and low aqueous solubility), oteseconazole is unlikely to be a MATE substrate. However, there is insufficient evidence to support this assertion. Where the potential role of MATE in the urinary excretion of oteseconazole is concerned, human mass balance study results indicated that 26% of radiolabeled oteseconazole was recovered in urine. However, the fraction of parent drug subject to active renal secretion is unknown. Nonetheless, because the mass balance study results suggest biliary excretion as the primary elimination pathway for oteseconazole, the contribution of MATE1 transporter to the biliary excretion of oteseconazole cannot be ruled out.
L788: The Applicant further indicated that the lack of significant inhibitory activity for OCT2 in vitro suggested that oteseconazole is unlikely to inhibit MATE. Although MATE inhibitors often overlap with OCT2 inhibitors, there are exceptions such as GAVRETOTM (pralsetinib), which inhibits MATE1 and MATE2-K but does not inhibit OCT1/2. Given that the potential for oteseconazole drug interactions with MATE substrates and inhibitors cannot be discounted, the review team has recommended and obtained the Applicant's concurrence for a post marketing evaluation of oteseconazole as a substrate and inhibitor of MATEs (MATE1 and MATE2-K) in vitro.
L789: Please see below for details of drug-drug interaction studies.
L790: ## Effect of Other Drugs on Oteseconazole
L791: ## Cytochrome P450 Inhibitors and Inducers
L792: Oteseconazole may be co-administered with cytochrome (CYP) P450 isoenzyme inhibitors and inducers without dosage adjustment based on the observation that oteseconazole is stable in human liver microsomes in vitro.
L793: ## Cell Membrane Transporter Inhibitors
L794: Oteseconazole may be co-administered with inhibitors of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT2, OAT1 and OAT3 without dosage adjustment based on in vitro study results.
L795: ## Effects of Oteseconazole on Other Drugs
L796: It is unlikely that oteseconazole would have impact on the PK of the concomitant medications that are the substrate of major CYP enzymes (i.e., CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6), or that are the substrate of major cell membrane transporters (i.e., P-gp, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT2, OAT1, and OAT3) because oteseconazole did not impact the metabolism of probe substrates for above mentioned CYP enzymes, or inhibit above mentioned cell membrane transporters at clinically relevant concentrations.
L797: ## CYP3A4 Substrates
L798: In clinical drug interaction study (VMT-VT-1161-CL-007), oteseconazole did not cause clinically significant inhibition of midazolam metabolism and caused weak induction of CYP3A4 based on around 36% decrease in AUC of midazolam. However, it is to be noted that the dosage regimen of oteseconazole used in this study was 600 mg once daily for 14
L799: consecutive days, but the proposed maintenance dose is 150 mg weekly. Therefore, low to no induction is expected at the recommended dose. Also, no clinically significant differences in pharmacokinetics of oral contraceptives of ethinyl estradiol and norethindrone, CYP3A4 substrate, were observed when administered concomitantly with oteseconazole (see details below). Therefore, the review team accepted the Applicant's proposal that no dosage adjustment is needed when CYP3A4 substrates are co-administered with oteseconazole.
L800: ## Substrates of P-gp and BCRP
L801: Clinical drug interaction study (VMT-VT-1161-CL-015) showed that digoxin C_{max} and area under the curve from 0 to 24 hours (AUC_{0-24h}) were similar (differences in mean ratios were approximately 6-13%) upon coadministration of digoxin with oteseconazole. However, coadministration of a single dose of 150 mg oteseconazole increased rosuvastatin C_{max}, AUC_{0-24h}, and area under the curve from zero to infinity (AUC_{0-\infty}) approximately 84% to 87%; and coadministration of 150 mg oteseconazole for 14 days increased rosuvastatin C_{max}, AUC_{0-24h} and AUC_{0-\infty} approximately 94% to 118%. The review team agrees with the Applicant that oteseconazole inhibits BCRP but does not inhibit P-gp. However, the review team disagrees with the Applicant's proposed labeling language, [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L802: (b) (4) Instead, based on the mitigation strategy recommended for approved drugs which also have drug interaction with rosuvastatin, increase rosuvastatin's systemic exposure at a similar level, and increased creatinine kinase in clinical studies, the review team recommends using the lowest possible starting dose or consider reducing the dose of drugs that are BCRP substrates and monitor for adverse reactions.
L803: ## Combined Oral Contraceptive
L804: When a combined oral contraceptive containing ethinyl estradiol (EE) and norethindrone (NE) was co-administered with oteseconazole in a clinical drug interaction study, EE C_{max}, AUC_{0-24h}, AUC_{0-t}, and AUC_{0-\infty}, were increased approximately 12% to 20% and NE C_{max}, AUC_{0-t}, and AUC_{0-\infty}, were similar (difference of 3% to 7%) compared to when administered alone. Therefore, no clinically meaningful effect of the concomitant use of oral contraceptives with oteseconazole would be anticipated, and thus no dosage adjustment is needed.
L805: ## 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L806: The oteseconazole IND had an agreed initial pediatric study plan that contained plans to request a partial waiver in pediatric patients less than 12 years of age as well as all male patients and to enroll female patients 12 to less than 18 years of age. Eligibility criteria for the phase 3 trials included postmenarchal females ages 12 years old and above; however, only 3 adolescent subjects enrolled into the phase 3 trials. Based on the preclinical findings and the review team's decision to contraindicate the use of oteseconazole in females who are not of reproductive potential, studies in adolescents will be waived. This will be addressed in the appropriate sections of the oteseconazole US prescribing information.
L807: Oteseconazole was presented to the Pediatric Review Committee on December 14, 2021. The PeRC agreed with the Division's recommendation to grant a full waiver for pediatric studies in the above-mentioned groups for the following reasons:
L808: - Less than 12 years of age and males of all ages because studies are impossible or highly impracticable
- For females &gt;12 to &lt;17 years of age because the product will be unsafe for use in this age group.
L809: ## 8.4. Pregnancy and Lactation
L810: ## Animal Data
L811: The nonclinical information in Table 54 was used in support of the indicated labeling sections. Additional nonclinical data are located in Sections 13.1 and 13.2, and the final labeling.
L812: **Table 54. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Pregnancy and Lactation**
L813: Labeling Section	Nonclinical data
8.1 Pregnancy	Ocular abnormalities including cataracts, opacities, exophthalmos, bupthalmos, optic nerve atrophy, retinal atrophy, lens degeneration and hemorrhage were observed in the offspring of pregnant rats administered oteseconazole from Gestation Day 6 through Lactation Day 20 at 7.5 mg/kg day (about 3.5 times the recommended human dose based on AUC comparisons). Opacities were consistently observed in pups after oteseconazole dosing of pregnant dams from the three rat strains tested: Crl:CD(SD), Crl:WI(Han) and Hsd:SD.
8.2 Lactation	There are no data available regarding the presence of oteseconazole in animal milk. It is unclear if the ocular abnormalities observed in a pre and postnatal studies resulted from pre-natal exposure or exposure via milk or both.
8.3 Females of reproductive potential	Ocular abnormalities including cataracts were observed in pre- and postnatal studies in three rat strains (see nonclinical data supporting Section 8.1 Pregnancy, above). These findings, along with a half-life of 138 days in humans, may indicate a risk to children exposed to oteseconazole in utero, even if the treatment with oteseconazole ended months before the patient became pregnant.
L814: Source:
L815: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the curve.
L816: A total of 29 pregnancies occurred during the three phase 3 and the supportive phase 2b dose ranging clinical trials: 20 oteseconazole exposed females and 9 placebo exposed females. Among the 20 oteseconazole exposed females who became pregnant, 9 were in the phase 2b trial, with 4 of 9 subjects receiving 150 mg x 3 months (low dose); 2 of 9 subjects receiving the 150 mg x 6 months regimen (low dose); 2 of 9 subjects receiving the 300 mg x 3 months regimen (high dose) and a single subject received the 300 mg x 6 months regimen (high dose).
L817: In light of the pre-and postnatal development ocular findings in rat pups, the Division consulted the Division of Pediatric and Maternal Health (DPMH) and the Division of Ophthalmology (DO) for input on the potential clinical implications of these nonclinical findings on oteseconazole labeling in pregnant and lactating females and females of reproductive potential.
L818: ## Pregnancy
L819: A summary of all pregnancy outcomes in the phase 1-3 trials are described below and a tabular summary of these pregnancies can be found in Section 17.5 in the Appendix.
L820: There were 24 documented pregnancies in 21 oteseconazole exposed subjects in the clinical trials for whom the following outcomes were reported:
L821: - **11 live births** (all healthy per the Applicant, 1 was preterm, 3 were Caesarean section [c/s] births)
- **7 elective terminations** (Subject (b)(6) in Trial CL-017 had two terminations; reasons for termination were not provided)
- **3 spontaneous miscarriages**1 subject in Trial CL-017 had a second pregnancy that resulted in a healthy live birth.
- 1 subject had a spontaneous miscarriage after completing 24 weeks of the investigational product.
- 1 subject had a chemical pregnancy. This pregnancy failed to implant after 8 weeks of exposure to oteseconazole.
- **1 subject** was in an ongoing pregnancy
- **2 subjects** were lost to follow-up
L822: In a response to an FDA IR, the Applicant confirmed that the 11 oteseconazole exposed live births were only followed up through birth; however, the infants were not followed up post-natally for any significant periods of time. The Applicant confirmed that they attempted to contact each reporting site and noted that eight infants were reported as being healthy with no known congenital anomalies; whereas, three patients did not respond to the follow-up contact.
L823: In their final consult evaluation to the Division, DPMH noted the following:
L824: **DPMH Comments:** Findings of cataracts in rats in the pre- and post-natal development study raise concerns for cataract development in humans. The available data on the use of oteseconazole during human pregnancy are limited to 11 exposed pregnancies that resulted in live births. At birth, none of the exposed infants had cataracts. When the Applicant contacted the study sites in September 2021, there were eight reported healthy infants and three infants that were lost to follow-up. The follow up time varied from six months to almost six years after birth. Although, this is reassuring, it is limited to a small number of cases.
L825: This reviewer notes that the pathogenesis of neonatal cataracts is not well understood and may occur at variable time periods depending on the cause. Therefore, it is unclear when the injury occurred in rats. Additionally, the 11 cases of human pregnancy exposure had variable exposure times and thus variable serum concentrations during the "sensitive period." Seven of the 11 patients received oteseconazole during the pre-pregnancy exposure window, three patients received oteseconazole during the first trimester, and one patient received oteseconazole during the 2nd and 3rd trimester.
L826: ## **Lactation**
L827: Of the eight patients that responded, six patients breastfed their infants, one did not, and the breastfeeding status of another patient was unknown. Of the six patients who breastfed their infants, four were currently breastfeeding their infants at the time of contact, and two breastfed their infants for a short duration (2 weeks and &lt;3 weeks).
L828: DPMH provided the following comments regarding oteseconazole and lactating females:
L829: **DPMH Comments:** *There are no data on whether oteseconazole is present in animal milk. Because oteseconazole has a long half-life and the rats were exposed during pregnancy and lactation, it is difficult to separate the time-period.*
L830: In his consult evaluation of the reported nonclinical findings to the review division, Dr. Wiley Chambers, the DO reviewer and Division Director noted that:
L831: **DO Comments:** *Because the likelihood of developing cataracts in humans is increased in drug products which cause cataracts in nonhumans, it is often recommended that clinical trials monitor for the potential development of cataracts. There have not been enough products which cause congenital cataracts to know whether this should be recommended with drug products that cause congenital cataracts. The clinical trials with this product did not report high numbers of subjects with cataracts, but the observation period of this trial was not likely long enough to evaluate cataract development. Cataract development due to drug products is often not noticed until 12-36 months after the administration of the drug product.*
L832: Based on the limitations of follow-up and the uncertainty of the nonclinical ocular findings, Dr. Chambers made the following conclusions and recommendations to the Division:
L833: *It is therefore not known whether the cataract findings in rats represent a risk to humans. In the absence of knowing the relevance of the finding, it is recommended that the finding be included in the package insert of the product.*
L834: *The likelihood of distinguishing the etiology of any cataract development in oteseconazole treated women or their offspring is very low. Additional studies in the oteseconazole treated women and their offspring is not recommended. To collect information on children born to mothers who have taken oteseconazole, the establishment of a registry of pregnant women who have taken oteseconazole should be considered.*
L835: Following consultation with DPMH and the Division of Ophthalmology, DAI concluded that administration of oteseconazole to pregnant and lactating females of reproductive potential should be contraindicated due to the ocular abnormalities observed in the offspring of pregnant rats dosed at 7.5 mg/kg/day during pre and postnatal development. In addition, the team decided to contraindicate use of oteseconazole in this population since they considered oteseconazole's terminal half-life sufficiently prolonged to preclude use of contraceptive products within a reasonable timeframe.
L836: ## 9. Product Quality
L837: The proposed drug product has been formulated as an immediate-release capsule containing 150 mg of oteseconazole drug substance. Oteseconazole capsules are lavender, hard gelatin, size 2 capsules printed with OTE 150 mg using black ink, proposed to be packaged in (b) (4) 18-count blister packages within a child-resistant carton. The NDA, as amended, has provided sufficient chemistry, manufacturing, and controls information to assure the identity, strength, purity, and quality of the proposed drug product, oteseconazole capsules. The manufacturing and testing facilities have been found acceptable and the Overall Manufacturing Inspection
L838: Recommendation of “Approve” was entered into Panorama by the Office of Pharmaceutical Manufacturing Assessment on October 5, 2021. Therefore, this NDA is recommended for approval by the Office of Pharmaceutical Quality; refer to the Office of Pharmaceutical Quality Integrated Assessment dated October 27, 2021, in DARRTS.
L839: The following Post-Marketing Commitment should be included in the action letter:
L840: Post-Marketing Commitment 4211-2: Submit the final reports and summaries of the currently ongoing ecotoxicity studies to support the categorical exclusion from an environmental assessment.

L844: ## Ethics
L845: All study protocols and amendments, as applicable, informed consent and assent (for ages 12 to 17 years) documents, and Investigator’s Brochure were approved by the appropriate Independent Ethics Committee, Institutional Review Board, or Research Ethics Board.
L846: All phase 3 trials were conducted in accordance with International Council for Harmonization and good clinical practice requirements, the most recent guiding principles of the Declaration of Helsinki, and recommendations outlined in FDA’s guidance for industry for (acute) vulvovaginal candidiasis (August 2019), as there are no existing FDA guidances for RVVC. The supportive phase 2b dose-ranging study was also conducted in accordance with the applicable ICH Guidelines and good clinical practice. Written informed consent or assent (for ages 12 to 17 years) was obtained from all subjects.
L847: ## Office of Scientific Investigations
L848: Routine PDUFA inspections were conducted for 7 clinical investigators: Drs. Gorgacz, Miller, Kasparian, Iglesias, Moraru, Maximos, and Mosher. The inspections covered three clinical studies performed under NDA 215888: VMT-VT-1161-CL-011 (Trial CL-011), VMT-VT-1161-CL-012 (Trial CL-012), and VMT-VT-1161-CL-017 (Trial CL-017).
L849: During their inspections, the inspectors evaluated primary efficacy endpoint source data (i.e., the investigator’s assessments of VVC signs and symptoms and mycology culture test results through Week 48 for Trials CL-011 and CL-012 and through Week 50 for Trial CL-017) which were then verified against the Applicant’s data line listings for all subjects randomized at these 7 selected sites.
L850: Several discrepancies in the investigator's total composite VVC signs and symptoms score were noted at 5 of the 7 sites (i.e., Drs. Gorgacz, Miller, Kasparian, Iglesias, and Moraru). In addition, certified copies of the mycology culture results from the central laboratories were verified against the Applicant's data line listings for all subjects randomized at these 7 sites. One discrepancy in the mycology culture test results was noted at Dr. Gorgacz's site. These discrepancies (in the investigator's total composite VVC signs and symptoms scores and the mycology culture test results) would have no impact on the overall efficacy results of the three trials.
L851: Two data reliability concerns related to the following were identified during the inspections:
L852: - **Accidental unblinding events:** The Office of Scientific Investigations review team determined that accidental unblinding events were noted to have occurred as a result of an Interactive Web Response System (IWRS) programming error, impacting 6 subjects enrolled in Trials CL-011 and CL-012 at two of the 7 sites inspected (i.e., Drs. Miller and Kasparian). The Applicant stated that this IWRS programming error resulted in the unblinding of the subjects' treatment assignment for 24 subjects at 16 different sites who were enrolled between 3 Oct to 27 Nov 2018. The IWRS service provider reportedly corrected the IWRS programming error on 28 November 2018. The unblinded site staff at the 16 different sites continued to perform their delegated study-related duties. Based on these findings, the Office of Scientific Investigations review team recommended that a sensitivity analysis be conducted with regard to the efficacy data generated from the 24 subjects noted in the Applicant's 13 Sep 2021 response in order to determine the robustness of the reported overall efficacy results. Refer to Section 16.4 for the sensitivity analyses.
- **Subject ineligibility:** Inspectors identified that at one site (Dr. Iglesias' site), there were 4 subjects (i.e., Subjects [REDACTED](b)(6)) who did not meet core inclusion criteria to support a history of recurrent VVC and/or diagnosis of acute VVC. These ineligible subjects were not identified during the Applicant's routine monitoring visits and were not reported to FDA in the listing of protocol deviations in the Clinical Study Report (i.e., Appendix) or the BIMO data line listing of protocol deviations (i.e., listing 7.1). Of note, according to the Clinical Study Report, all of these subjects were excluded from the modified intent-to-treat population and the per-protocol population except for Subject [REDACTED](b)(6). This subject was included in the per-protocol population but should have been excluded.
L853: In their concluding remarks, the Office of Scientific Investigations review team stated, "Notwithstanding the ineligible subjects, unblinding events, and recommendations noted above, the data otherwise generated by these sites appear acceptable in support of the respective indication."
L854: ## Financial Disclosure Concerns
L855: The Applicant did not identify any financial disclosure concerns that would potentially affect the interpretation of safety or efficacy for any of their phase 3 trials.

L859: ## 12. Summary of Regulatory History
L860: On March 08, 2011, Viamet Pharmaceuticals, Inc. (Viamet) submitted a request for a Pre-Investigational New Drug (PIND) meeting to discuss the development of VT-1161 capsules (b) (4). The subsequent meeting package included a synopsis of the initial clinical protocol entitled, “A Phase 1, Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Dose-Escalation Study to Evaluate the Safety, Tolerability, and Pharmacokinetics of Single, Oral Doses of VT-1161 in Healthy Adult Subjects.” The Division granted a June 03, 2011, meeting and meeting minutes were issued on June 27, 2011.
L861: On June 30, 2011, Viamet submitted IND 111675 for VT-1161 capsules (b) (4). The IND contained study protocol VMT-VT-1161-CL-001, entitled, “A Phase 1, Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Dose-Escalation Study to Evaluate the Safety, Tolerability and Pharmacokinetics of Single Oral Doses of VT-1161 in Healthy Adult Subjects.” There were no clinical hold issues and the Division issued comments on their review of this initial IND on August 19, 2011.
L862: On December 21, 2012, Viamet submitted final study reports for study VMT-VT-1161-CL-002, entitled “A Phase 1, Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Dose-Escalation Study to Evaluate the Safety, Tolerability and Pharmacokinetics of Multiple Oral Doses of VT-1161 in Healthy Adult Subjects” and for study VMT-V-1161-CL-001, entitled “A Phase 1, Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Dose-Escalation Study to Evaluate the Safety, Tolerability and Pharmacokinetics of Single Oral Doses of VT-1161 in Healthy Adult Subjects.” The submission also contained a request for an End-of-Phase 1 (EOPI) meeting to discuss further development of VT-1161 and to obtain feedback on two phase 2a study protocols.
L863: In the meeting request, Viamet proposed (b) (4) a phase 2 study in vulvovaginal candidiasis (VVC). The EOPI meeting occurred on March 26, 2013. In the meeting minutes the Division noted their disagreement with some design elements of the proposed phase 2 studies. The Division also advised Viamet (b) (4).
L864: On July 3, 2013, Viamet submitted protocol VMT-VT-1161-004 entitled, “A Phase 2a, Randomized, Double-Blind, Dose-Ranging Study to Evaluate the Efficacy and Safety of VT-1161 Oral Tablets Compared to Fluconazole in the Treatment of Patients with Moderate to Severe Acute Vulvovaginal Candidiasis.” The Division issued comments on August 09, 2013, noting that the study was considered purely exploratory and that more rigorous statistical testing would be needed for a more definitive trial.
L865: On April 02, 2015, the Division issued comments on Viamet’s submissions from November 21, 2014, and January 16, 2015, that included a protocol for Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-006 entitled, “A Phase 2, Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Dose-Ranging Study to Evaluate the Efficacy and Safety of VT-1161 Oral Tablets in the Treatment of Patients with Recurrent
L866: Vulvovaginal Candidiasis.” The Division noted that the proposed trial was considered purely exploratory and that adequate control of the type I error and pre-specification of formal statistical hypothesis was expected for more definitive trials.
L867: On September 08, 2016, Viamet requested a type C meeting to discuss the clinical development program for VT-1161 for the treatment of recurrent vulvovaginal candidiasis (RVVC) including the design of their phase 3 trials. In the comments and final meeting minutes for the December 09, 2016, meeting, the Division noted their disagreement with the proposed design of a phase 3 trial.
L868: On September 27, 2016, Viamet was granted Fast Track (FT) and QIDP designations for treatment of VVC and treatment of recurrent vulvovaginal candidiasis.
L869: On May 11, 2017, Viamet requested a type B, End-of-Phase 2 meeting, to discuss the proposed phase 3 development plans for VT-1161 for the treatment of RVVC. The meeting was held on May 11, 2017. In their comments the Division noted that overall, the proposed phase 3 trial design was acceptable.
L870: The Applicant submitted an initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) on October 16, 2017. The iPSP was reviewed by the Pediatric Review Committee on December 13, 2017, and comments were issued to the Viamet on December 20, 2017.
L871: On March 15, 2018, Viamet Pharmaceuticals, Inc. informed FDA that the name of the company had changed to Mycovia Pharmaceuticals, Inc. (Applicant) effective January 2018, for oteseconazole capsule (previously identified as VT-1161 capsule; referred to as Vivjoa (oteseconazole) in this review).

L885: ## 13. Pharmacology Toxicology: Additional Information and Assessment
L886: ## 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted Under the IND
L887: ## 13.1.1. Primary Pharmacology
L888: ## Study VMT-SU-1: Sterol 14\alpha-demethylase (CYP51) Inhibition by Four New Antifungal Compounds VT-1129, VT-1148, VT-1161 and VT-1163: An In
L889: ## Vitro Study Utilizing Recombinant *Candida albicans* and Human CYP51 Proteins.
L890: ## **Key Study Findings**
L891: Oteseconazole, at 0.004 µg/ml, was shown to inhibit sterol 14-demethylation in two strains of *Candida albicans* and almost completely (94-97%) block formation of ergosterol, leading to the accumulation of lanosterol and other toxic methylated steroids.
L892: - No discernible ligand binding was obtained for oteseconazole with Human CYP51 at drug concentrations up to 86µM
- No discernible inhibition of HuCYP51 activity was obtained with oteseconazole at drug concentrations up to 50µM.

L906: ## 13.1.2. Secondary Pharmacology
L907: ## Study # 17319: In Vitro Study of 4 Compounds
L908: ## **Key Study Findings**
L909: - The human melatonin MT1 receptor is among the receptors most potently inhibited by oteseconazole.
L910: - Since published literature have shown that melatonin can play a role in cataract formation (Benitez and Carver 2019) and that melatonin receptors are present in juvenile rats (Alkozi et al. 2020), adverse eye findings in the pre and postnatal studies could theoretically involve oteseconazole inhibition of this receptor.
L911: **Table 58. In Vitro Pharmacology: Binding Assays for Select Receptors Inhibited by 10µM Oteseconazole (by Greater Than 50%)**
L912: Receptor	% Inhibition
Human adenosine A3 receptor	93 %
Human melatonin MT1 receptor	81%
Rat cerebral cortex Na+-channel (site 2)	61
Rat cerebral cortex Cl--channel (GABA-gated)	97%
Human norepinephrine transporter,	65 %
Human dopamine transporter	71 %
L913: Source: Reviewer generated table.
L914: Abbreviations: Cl, calcium ion; GABA, gamma aminobutyric acid; Na, sodium.
L915: Human melatonin MT1 receptors are among the receptors most potently inhibited by oteseconazole in this screening assay. Published literature have demonstrated melatonin receptors throughout the body including the retina and these receptors may have a role in regulating fluid pressure inside of the eye. Melatonin and analogues decrease intraocular pressure in both normotensive and hypertensive eyes (Alkozi et al. 2020). Melatonin receptors may also contribute to cataract formation (Khorsand et al. 2016).
L916: Since MT1 melatonin receptors have been shown to be present in the juvenile rat (Ting et al. 1999), oteseconazole activity at the melatonin receptor could contribute to adverse eye findings such as exophthalmos and cataracts observed in the pre and postnatal studies in rats.
L917: ## **Study XT148002: In Vitro Evaluation of VT-1161 and VT-464 as Inhibitors and Substrates of Human P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT2, OAT1 and OAT3 Transporters**
L918: The ability of VT-1161 (2.5 and 25 µM) to inhibit human efflux transporters, P-glycoprotein (P-gp) (MDR1/ABCB1) and breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP) (ABCG2) was evaluated by measuring the bidirectional permeability of a probe substrate (digoxin or prazosin) across a monolayer of Caco-2 and MDCKII-BCRP cells in the presence of VT-1161. Subsequently, the IC50 for the inhibition of P-gp and BCRP was determined using VT-1161 concentrations of 0.1, 0.3, 1, 3, 10 and 25 µM. VT-1161 inhibited the human efflux transporter BCRP with a half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) of 0.4 µM.

L928: ## 13.1.4. General Toxicology
L929: ## Study 2139-002: A 26-Week Oral Toxicity Study in Rats With a 17-Week Recovery Period.
L930: Oteseconazole dosing for 26 weeks in rats resulted in changes in the liver, adrenals, thyroid, kidneys, and ovaries with no significant effects at 0.15 mg/kg and minimal, nonadverse findings representing an extension of the drug's pharmacology or of questionable human relevance (thyroid follicular cell hypertrophy/hyperplasia, hepatocellular vacuolation, hepatocellular hypertrophy and renal tubular pigment) at 0.5 mg/kg day, about 0.2 times the maximum recommended human dosage (MRHD) based on area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) comparisons.
L931: At doses \ge 1.5 mg/kg/day (about 1.3 times the MRHD based on AUC comparisons, additional findings in rats included increased cholesterol, globulin, platelet counts, mild, reversible prolongations in APTT, increased organ weights (liver, thyroid, adrenal), adrenal gland vacuolation, chronic progressive nephropathy, hepatocellular hypertrophy/hyperplasia, multinucleated hepatocytes, increased Kupffer cell pigment, sex cord/stromal hyperplasia with (at 5 mg/kg) decreased prostate weight, increased lung histiocytosis, hepatocellular necrosis, increases cytochrome P450 content (+100%), Thyroxine (+525%) and Triiodothyroxine glucuronidation (+265 %).
L932: At the end of the 17-week recovery period, increased platelet count, decreased reticulocytes and thyroid follicular hyperplasia were observed at doses \ge 1.5 mg/kg/day while tubular pigment, hepatocellular hypertrophy, hepatocellular necrosis, multinucleated hepatocytes and pigmented Kupffer cells persisted only at 5 mg/kg (about 6 times the MRHD based on AUC comparisons). The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) was considered to be 0.5 mg/kg which was associated with a maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}) of 741 ng/mL and an AUC of 14,295 ng\cdothr/mL (about 0.2 times the MRHD based on AUC comparisons).

Abbreviations: NE, not evaluated
L953: After 26 weeks, mild, statistically significant prolongations in APTT were present in both sexes administered ≥1.50 mg/kg/day (up to +46%; prolonged by up to 7.74 seconds), relative to controls. Prolongations in APTT indicated reduced blood coagulability. A mild, statistically significant prolongation in prothrombin time was also noted in males administered 5 mg/kg/day (+12%; prolonged 1.96 seconds). No alterations in coagulation times were noted in recovery animals.
L954: The toxicological significance of pigment deposits in the kidneys and liver is unclear. Since red blood cells are broken down by phagocytic action of Kupffer cells, where the hemoglobin molecule is split, brown pigment deposits were interpreted by the Applicant to be hemosiderin. Special stains, (Hall's bile and iron kit, Prussian blue reaction), were used on selected control and high dose animals from both sexes to further characterize the pigment present in hepatocytes, Kupffer cells and the renal tubules. Since the pigment was negative for bile and iron, the identity of the pigment remains unknown.
L955: Microscopic lesions in the kidney at terminal necropsy included tubular pigment and exacerbation of the chronic progressive nephropathy. Chronic progressive nephropathy is a common background finding in rats and was present in all dose groups including the control group at terminal necropsy. There was an increase in the incidence/severity in both sexes at 1.5
L956: and 5 mg/kg/day. The incidence/severity of chronic progressive nephropathy was reduced at the end of the recovery period compared to the end of dosing and was comparable to control at 1.5 mg/kg. The drug-induced exacerbation of chronic progressive nephropathy in rats may not be predictive of human toxicity since chronic progressive nephropathy has no counterpart in humans.
L957: The significance of persistent multinucleated hepatocytes is unclear, but it was only seen in the high dose rats, is believed to be a rat-specific finding and is also seen with drugs such as rifabutin (Scampini et al. 1993). Multinucleated hepatocytes may result from the fusion of hepatocytes (Hayashi et al. 2008). There were no biologically significant increases in liver tumors in the two-year rat study of oteseconazole.

L963: ## Study # 2130-001. A 39-Week Oral Toxicity Study of VT-1161 in Dogs With a 33-Week Recovery
L964: ## Key Study Findings
L965: - Dogs were dosed with oteseconazole for 39 weeks at 0.75, 1.5, 5.5 and 17 mg/kg, equivalent to between 1 and 26 times the MRHD based on AUC comparisons
- Reversible effects seen at all doses were consistent with the pharmacology (impaired steroidogenesis) and included enlarged adrenals, increased adrenal weights, adrenal hypertrophy/hyperplasia, vacuolation, mononuclear cell infiltration increased cholesterol and reduced prostate weights. Adrenal gland 'degeneration' was observed in recovery animals at doses \ge5.5 mg/kg/day
L966: - Adrenal cortical vacuoles, seen as multilamellar structures in TEM may reflect phospholipidosis, which is seen with other azoles and would not be considered adverse.
- The NOAEL was considered to be 17 mg/kg since the findings represented either an extension of the pharmacology of oteseconazole or possibly phospholipidosis which was associated with a C_{max} of 79,400 ng/mL and an AUC of 1,675,000 ng·hr/mL (about 26 times the MRHD based on AUC comparisons).

L976: At recovery, minimal to moderate 'degeneration' of the adrenal gland cortex was present in one male at 17.0 mg/kg/day and in all females at 5.5 and 17.0 mg/kg/day. According to the Applicant, "*the degeneration consisted of cortical cells with vacuolated cytoplasm and either pyknotic or absent nuclei.*" Vacuolation is a prominent feature of phospholipidosis and pyknotic nuclei are often found in the adrenal gland. Since the individual animal finding was recorded as 'degeneration', it was not clear how many animals actually showed absent nuclei and if this degeneration is in fact phospholipidosis. Phospholipidosis has been observed with other azole antifungals.

L982: ## 13.1.7. Reproductive Toxicology
L983: ## Study 2130-009: VT-1161: An Oral Study of Male Fertility and Early
## Embryonic Development in Rats
L984: ## Key Study Findings
L985: - There was no adverse effect on male reproductive function at 0.5 mg/kg/day, about 0.2 times the MRHD based on AUC comparisons.
- Increased abnormal sperm morphology, % sperm with detached head and % sperm with no hook were detected at 3 mg/kg/day, (about 1.4 time the MRHD based on AUC) and higher.
- Reduced sperm motility and sperm count were detected at 10 mg/kg/day about 7 times the MRHD based on AUC comparisons. Sperm concentration remained slightly reduced (15%) at the end of a 12-week recovery period.

The day on which positive evidence of copulation was observed was considered GD 0 and on GD 13, each female was euthanized, and the uterus and ovaries examined. Upon completion of most of the GD 13 uterine examinations, the first 10 main study males from the 0 and 10 mg/kg/day groups, and any surviving main study males from the 0.5 and 3 mg/kg/day groups were observed externally, euthanized, and subjected to a necropsy. During Week 23, the remaining (recovery) males were observed externally, euthanized, and subjected to a necropsy. Sperm analysis was conducted.

L994: ## Study 2130-008: An Oral Study of Fertility and Early Embryonic Development in Female Rats
L995: ## **Key Study Findings**
L996: - Maternal toxicity consisted of transient reductions in premating bodyweight change at 5 and 25 mg/kg and gestational bodyweight changes at 25 mg/kg.
- Fertility index, percent post-implantation loss and mean number or early and late resorptions were numerically and statistically different from concurrent controls but were within the range of historical control values.
- The NOAEL for maternal toxicity was 1.5 mg/kg and the NOAEL for fertility and embryo-fetal development was 25 mg/kg, with an AUC estimated to be 898,600 ng*h/mL, equivalent to about 14 times the MRHD based on AUC comparisons.

Formulation/vehicle:	0.5% (w/w) Carboxymethylcellulose (CMC: medium viscosity) in deionized water
Species/strain:	Rattus norvegicus/ CD® [Crl:CD® (SD)]
Number/sex/group:	25
Satellite groups:	None
Study design:	Female rats were dosed with oteseconazole beginning 28 days prior to pairing through GD 7. On Day 29, each female was housed in the cage of an untreated male from the same group. Positive evidence of copulation was established by a copulatory plug in the vagina and/or the presence of sperm in the vaginal lavage. The day on which evidence of copulation was observed was considered GD 0. The female was returned to an individual cage for the remainder of the study. On GD 13, each female was euthanized, the uterus and ovaries were exposed and the number of normally developing embryos, resorptions, and the total number of implantations were recorded. The number of corpora lutea on each ovary was also recorded.
Deviation from study protocol affecting interpretation of results:	No

L1065: ## 13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted to the NDA
L1066: ## 13.2.1. Carcinogenicity
L1067: ## Study 2130-018: A 104-Week Oral Carcinogenicity Study in Rats.
L1068: ## Key Study Findings
L1069: - Excess mortality was observed in rats dosed at 5 mg/kg and the high dose was subsequently reduced to 3 mg/kg at Week 33 for males and Week 55 for females. Based on high mortality, all males were terminated between Weeks 77 (5/3 mg/kg) and 90 and all females were terminated between Weeks 84-86.
- Hemorrhage was increased in the adrenals, brain, coagulating gland, ears, epididymis, head, heart, lung, nose pancreas, prostate, seminal vesicles, spinal cord, testes, thymus, and urinary bladder in male rats with a few cases of hemorrhage in the brain, lung, and pancreas in females.
- Statistically significant increases in thyroid follicular cell adenomas and combined thyroid follicular cell adenoma and carcinoma in both males and females, at 1.5 mg/kg and above (AUC exposure similar to the exposure at recommended human dose) and thyroid follicular cell carcinoma in HD females at 5 mg/kg/day, (AUC exposure about 7 times the exposure at the recommended human dose)

L1078: ## Discussion
L1079: Thyroid follicular cell tumors are not uncommon in drugs with increased T3 turnover by the liver and these may be secondary to chronic thyroid follicular cell stimulation (Hood et al. 1999). In male and female rats administered oteseconazole at 5 mg/kg/day for 26-weeks, there were 4- to 8-fold increases in liver T4 glucuronidation activities and 2- to 5-fold T3 glucuronidation activities compared to concurrent control animals. The rat is believed to be highly sensitive to the development of thyroid follicular cell tumors as this species lacks T4-binding globulin, the high affinity plasma protein which carries 70% of human T4 and T3. Instead, the rat relies upon the lower affinity transthyretin and albumin as a buffer for alterations in thyroid hormone concentrations. This reduced buffering capacity in the rat causes greater fluctuations in free T4 and T3 and therefore a greater sensitivity for TSH release when levels of these hormones are reduced. There were no thyroid tumors in Tg.rasH2 mice exposed to oteseconazole for 26 weeks. The development of thyroid tumors secondary to selective induction of liver metabolism appears to be unique to the rat and may have reduced relevance in predicting human carcinogenicity (Bartsch et al. 2018). The human relevance of the increase in thyroid follicular cell tumors is unclear.
L1080: The Applicant also takes the position that the development of Leydig cell tumors in the rat is a result of inhibition of testosterone by oteseconazole and a consequent release from feedback inhibition on luteinizing hormone release from the pituitary resulting in overstimulation of the Leydig cells and development of hyperplasia and adenoma. This mechanism is considered plausible as compounds which interfere with testosterone synthesis are associated with an increase in luteinizing hormone levels and the development of this tumor. Support for this mechanism in rats administered oteseconazole was directly tested in study No. 2130-019. A decrease in group mean serum testosterone concentrations (-80%) and increase in luteinizing hormone was observed at 100 mg/kg/day which correlated with changes in the testes, prostate and seminal vesicles/ coagulating glands generally associated with testosterone deficiency.
L1081: Hemorrhage was increased in the adrenals, brain, coagulating gland, ears, epididymis, head, heart, lung, nose pancreas, prostate, seminal vesicles, spinal cord, testes, thymus, and urinary bladder in male rats with a few cases of hemorrhage in the brain, lung, and pancreas in females. In contrast, there was no drug-related increase in hemorrhage in the 39-week dog study and the 26-week rat study. and coagulation parameters were only mildly affected. The brain was within normal limits at 5 mg/kg in males and females at the end of 26 weeks in rats.
L1082: ## Study 2130-025. VT-1161: A 26-Week Oral Oncogenicity Study in CByB6F1-Tg(HRAS)2Jic[rasH2] Transgenic Mice
L1083: ## Key Study Findings
L1084: - There were no oteseconazole-related tumors in male and female TgrasH2 mice.
- TgrasH2 mice received once daily oral gavage doses of 0 (control), 0.5, 1.5, or 5 mg/kg/day (males) and 0 (control), 1.5, 5, or 15 mg/kg/day (females) oteseconazole in 0.5% carboxymethylcellulose for 26 weeks. There were no statistically significant increases in tumors compared to controls and no tumors which increased with a statistically significant dose response relationship in male or female mice.
L1085: ## 13.2.2. General Toxicology; Additional Studies
L1086: ## Study #2130-019: Effect of VT-1161 on the Male Reproductive System in Rats.
L1087: Table 91. Summary of Effects on Reproductive Hormones Measured by Enzyme-Linked Immunosorbent Assay and LC-MS/MS
