L1090: Abbreviations: DHEA, dehydroepiandrosterone; DHT, dihydrotestosterone; GLP, good laboratory practice; LC-MS/MS, liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry.
L1091: Groups of male and female Sprague Dawley rats were administered 30 or 100 oteseconazole by oral gavage once daily for 7 days. Oteseconazole doses of 30 or 100 mg/kg/day were associated with reproductive hormone changes at 30 and 100 mg/kg/day that suggested disrupted testosterone biosynthesis. Effects included decreased serum 17\alpha-hydroxyprogesterone, androstenedione (100 mg/kg/day only), testosterone and dihydrotestosterone concentrations, and a compensatory increase in luteinizing hormone and progesterone concentrations (100 mg/kg/day only). Serum follicle stimulating hormone concentration was also mildly increased at 100 mg/kg/day. Microscopic findings consistent with testosterone deficiency were observed in the male reproductive tract at 100 mg/kg/day.
L1092: ## 13.2.3. Reproductive Toxicology: Additional Studies
L1093: ## Study 01234002: A Study of Postnatal Ocular Development in Rat Offspring Following Maternal Oral Administration of VT-1161 During Pregnancy and Lactation.
L1094: ## Key Study Findings
L1095: - Increased drug-related mortality was observed post-weaning (postnatal day [PND] 21-23) in oteseconazole-treated animals associated with microchip implantation. This degree of excess mortality was not seen in a subsequent study at the same dose.
- Decreased erythrocyte mass (red blood cell count, hemoglobin, and hematocrit, with macrocytosis and anisocytosis) was observed in surviving F1 males and females on PND 24-25 with an increase in absolute reticulocyte count at PND 24-25 but hematology parameters were comparable to controls on PND 61.
- Cataracts were increased in drug-treated animals at all time points evaluated (PND 15-18, 26-30 and 50-55). Hemorrhage and hyphema were also observed in some animals.

L1109: ## Study 01234003: An Investigative Study of the Effects of VT-1161 on Postnatal Development in Rats
L1110: ## **Key Study Findings**
L1111: - Oteseconazole was administered to pregnant and lactating rats at 7.5 mg/kg/day which resulted in reduced bodyweights (up to -16%), lens opacities, swollen eyes, hemorrhage, hyphema, bupthalmos and corneal ulceration on PND 21 and PND 34. Postmortem findings included retinal and optic nerve atrophy as well as lens degeneration.
- Clinical chemistry changes on PND 21 included a markedly increased GGT titer, increased prothrombin time and APTT which reversed by PND 34
- Overall mortality in pups was comparable to control and reduced compared to Study 1234002

To reduce variability among the F1 litters, 8 pups/litter of equal sex distribution, if possible, were randomly selected on PND 7 with the exception that any pup observed with an abnormality of the eyes was retained. Following collection of blood samples for Hematology, Coagulation, and clinical chemistry, 2 pups per litter (1 pup/sex/litter, if possible) had whole carcasses retained in 10% neutral-buffered formalin for possible future histopathology. Remaining culled pups were discarded without further examination. Ocular examinations were conducted by a board-certified veterinary ophthalmologist during PND 20-21 and PND 33-34. On PND 21 approximately half of the pups selected to comprise the post-weaning F1 generation from each litter were identified with a subcutaneous microchip and the other half were identified with an ear tag, across both sexes as possible. Macroscopic necropsy, and a limited tissue microscopic pathology were collected for F1 animals.

L1129: Abbreviations: A/G, albumin/globulin; APTT, activated partial thromboplastin clotting time; GGT, gamma-glutamyl transpeptidase; HCT, hematocrit; HGB, hemoglobin; ND, not determined; PND, postnatal day; RBC, red blood cell, RDW, red cell distribution width; WBC, white blood cell.
L1130: Gamma-glutamyl transpeptidase (GGT) plays a key role in breaking down extracellular glutathione (GSH) and providing cysteine, the rate-limiting substrate, for intracellular *de novo* synthesis of GSH. GGT is found in high levels in the liver, kidney, pancreas, heart, and brain but elevated levels of GGT are generally interpreted to indicate damage to the liver and bile ducts. However, GGT activity is elevated in many diseases including cardiovascular disease, diabetes mellitus, and cancers. The expression of GGT is increased as an adaptive response during oxidative stress in order to facilitate GSH turnover, *de novo* GSH synthesis, and metabolism and detoxification of GSH conjugates (Zhang et al. 2005). The significance of the increased GGT levels in these pups remains unclear.
L1131: ## Study 01234004: An Investigative Study of the Effects of VT-1161 on Pre- and Postnatal Development in Hsd:SD and Wistar (Han) Rats
L1132: ## Key Study Findings
L1133: - There was a high incidence of lens opacity present in both Hsd:SD and Crl:WI(Han) rat strains at the oteseconazole dose of 7.5 mg/kg/day although there were subtle differences in the pattern of the findings.
- At PND 21, oteseconazole-related changes in both strains included altered coagulation (prolonged prothrombin time PT and APTT), hematology (mildly decreased red blood cell parameters including RBC count, hemoglobin and hematocrit, minimally decreased reticulocyte count, mildly increased platelet count, increased neutrophil count), and clinical chemistry (increased cholesterol and triglycerides and markedly increased GGT 15x and 5x in Hsd:SD and Wistar Han rats respectively).
- At PND 35, most changes had resolved but platelets and GGT remained elevated, although the values were reduced compared to Day 21 values.

L1149: ## Specifications for Impurities/Degradants
L1150: The limit for each individual related product was set at \le 0.2\%, consistent with International Conference on Harmonisation (ICH) Q3B(R2) based upon a maximum daily dose of 600 mg. All individual impurities have been detected at less than (b)(4)%.
L1151: All potential and actual impurities in oteseconazole with origins in the final manufacturing steps were classified according to ICH M7(R1) using in-silico methods unless otherwise noted, with some undergoing Ames testing. Most were classified as Class 5 but three were designated as Class 4 because the updated software (Derek 6.0.0 Nexus 2.2.0 (mutagenicity only) and Leadscope Model Applier version 2.3.7-1 (mutagenicity suite)) provided an alert for the trifluoroethoxy portion. Since the trifluoroethoxy portion is also contained in oteseconazole, which is negative in the Ames assay and earlier versions of the software did not provide alerts for this subunit, compounds with this structural feature were designated as class 5.

L1157: ## 14. Clinical Pharmacology: Additional Information and Assessment
L1158: ## 14.1. In Vitro Studies
L1159: ## 14.1.1. Plasma Protein Binding (MC11M-0008.1 and OPR-VMT-0449.01)
L1160: Study MC11M-0008.1 evaluated the extent of oteseconazole binding to mouse, rat, dog and human plasma proteins at concentrations of 0.26, 2.5, 26 and 105 µg/mL using equilibrium dialysis. Oteseconazole was observed to bind extensively to plasma proteins, with percentage bound of 99.5±0.058 and 99.7±0.058 in human plasma at concentrations of 0.26 and 2.5 µg/mL, respectively. The degree of protein binding decreased slightly to 98.2±1.07 and 98.5±0.35 at concentrations of 26 and 105 µg/mL, respectively, likely indicating saturation of binding to plasma proteins. Study OPR-VMT-0449.01 was a comparative assessment of plasma protein binding in human plasma for oteseconazole and 4 other antifungal agents (i.e., itraconazole, posaconazole, fluconazole and terbinafine) at a concentration of 5µM. Consistent with Study MC11M-0008.1, oteseconazole plasma protein binding was determined to be 99.8% at this concentration (which is equivalent to 2.64 µg/mL).
L1161: **Reviewer's Comment:** The range of C_{max} values associated with the proposed dosing regimen is 1.4 – 2.8 µg/mL. Therefore, the clinically relevant range of protein binding values for oteseconazole is 99.5 - 99.7%.
L1162: ## Drug-Drug Interactions
L1163: ## Cytochrome P450 Enzymes
L1164: In vitro studies indicated that oteseconazole is stable under the incubation conditions evaluated; little to no loss was observed in the incubations with human liver microsomes and minimal loss (&lt; 18%) was observed when incubated with various recombinant human cytochrome (CYP) P450 isoenzymes (i.e., CYP1A2, CYP2A6, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1 and CYP3A4/5). C1 (hydroxylation and glucuronide conjugation) was only formed by dog hepatocytes and no additional metabolites were formed by Sprague Dawley (SD) rat, Cynomolgus monkey and human hepatocyte.
L1165: **Reviewer's Comments:** The reviewer agrees that no clinical DDI studies using strong index inhibitors and/or inducers of CYP enzymes are needed. Based on the above findings and absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion (ADME) study results [where no metabolites were identified in pooled plasma samples], metabolism is not expected to contribute significantly to the elimination of oteseconazole.
L1166: In vitro evaluation of oteseconazole as an inhibitor of CYP450 enzymes involved incubation of oteseconazole at concentrations ranging from 0.01 to 10 µM in pooled human liver microsomes with metabolic substrates at their determined K_m concentrations. Following a 5-minute incubation, reactions were quenched with acetonitrile and substrate metabolites were analyzed by liquid chromatography with tandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS). Study results indicate that
L1167: Oteseconazole is a direct, reversible inhibitor of several CYP isoforms in the following order of decreasing inhibitory potency: CYP2C8 (IC_{50} = 1.4 \muM) &gt; CYP2B6 (IC_{50} = 4.0 \muM) &gt; CYP2C19 (IC_{50} = 9.7 \muM), CYP2D6 (IC_{50} = 9.4 \muM), CYP3A (as measured by testosterone 6\beta-hydroxylation, IC_{50} = 9.6 \muM) &gt; CYP2C9 (IC_{50} &gt; 10 \muM), CYP3A (as measured by midazolam 1'-hydroxylation, IC_{50} &gt; 10 \muM). Oteseconazole did not inhibit CYP1A2 within the range of concentrations evaluated. Additionally, oteseconazole showed little to no time- or metabolism-dependent inhibition of any of the tested CYP isoforms.
L1168: **Reviewer's Comments:** In the inhibition study, 10 \muM of oteseconazole was the upper end of the concentration range evaluated due to solubility limitations.
L1169: Although the activities of CYP2C9 and CYP3A (with midazolam as the substrate) were reduced by approximately 48% and 42%, respectively, IC_{50} values for CYP3A and CYP2C9 reported as &gt;10 \muM.
L1170: The ability of oteseconazole to induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4/5 enzymes was evaluated in vitro in primary cultures of cryopreserved human hepatocytes. Induction was assessed by measuring activity and expression of the CYP enzymes after treatment with oteseconazole (10 \muM). The study results indicate that treatment with up to 10 \muM oteseconazole caused increases (&gt; 2-fold change and/or &gt; 20% of the positive control) in CYP1A2, CYP2B6 and CYP3A4/5 activity and CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19 and CYP3A4 mRNA levels in at least 2 hepatocyte cultures.
L1171: **Reviewer's Comments:** In summary, based on in vitro study results, oteseconazole has shown both inhibition and induction potential for all tested CYP enzymes, except for CYP1A2. Oteseconazole showed only induction effect on CYP1A2.
L1172: Following completion of these studies, the Applicant assessed the clinical relevance of the in vitro CYP inhibition and induction data by comparing the inhibition/induction potency to steady-state plasma concentrations of oteseconazole achieved in humans at clinically relevant doses. The first step was a basic analysis that considered only the in vitro inhibitory or induction potency and the highest anticipated C_{max} in humans, based on total drug (protein bound + unbound) in plasma at steady state. If the analysis suggested that a drug-drug interaction (DDI) may possibly occur in vivo, a second analysis utilizing a mechanistic static model was then performed.
L1173: Based on the basic, step 1 analysis for evaluation of CYP inhibition potential, a DDI study would be recommended for each of the CYP isoforms evaluated in the CYP inhibition study except for CYP1A2. Based on the basic, step 1 analysis for evaluation of CYP induction potential, a DDI study would be recommended for each of the isoforms tested.
L1174: **Reviewer's Comments:** Per the FDA guidance for industry *In Vitro Drug Interaction Studies — Cytochrome P450 Enzyme- and Transporter-Mediated Drug Interactions* (January 2020), the recommended equation for the calculation of the R1 value for basic model of reversible inhibition is R1 = 1 + (I_{max}, u/Ki, u), where I_{max}, u is the maximal unbound plasma concentration of the interacting drug at steady state, and Ki, u is the unbound inhibition constant determined in vitro. For Step 1, the basic model analysis, the Applicant used the formula R1 = 1 + [I]/Ki, where [I] is the maximum total concentration (protein bound + unbound) of the inhibitor in plasma at steady state in humans receiving a therapeutic dose of the drug; and Ki is
L1175: the inhibition constant of the inhibitor for the CYP isoform in question. Also, the guidance recommends 1.02 for the cutoff value (R_1), however, the Applicant used the cutoff value of 1.1 for R_1. Additionally, the formula used for basic model analysis for CYP induction is also slightly different from the guidance, which is R_3 = 1 / \{1 + (d \cdot E_{max} \cdot [I]) / (EC_{50} + [I])\}, while the formula in the guidance is R_3 = 1 / [1 + d \times ((E_{max} \times 10 \times I_{max,u}) / (EC_{50} + 10 \times I_{max,u}))]. The cutoff for R_3 used by Applicant is 0.9 whereas the guidance recommends cutoff value of R_3 is 0.8. Although the validity of the Applicant's approach cannot be verified, the Applicant's conclusions suggest a worst-case scenario of a potential for clinically relevant DDIs with all CYP isoforms. Therefore, from a risk assessment perspective, the reviewer agrees with the Applicant's decision to further evaluate the need for clinical DDI studies.
L1176: To further evaluate the need for clinical DDI studies, refine these decisions, a step 2 analysis was performed by the Applicant using a mechanistic static model, which predicts the in vivo area under the curve ratio (AUCR). If AUCR is &gt; 1.25, this indicates net inhibition and if AUCR is &lt; 0.8, this indicates net induction. If either of these conditions are met, an in vivo human DDI study is recommended. The results are shown in Table 118 below. The study results suggest that all AUCR values are within the range of 0.8 and 1.25, and thus there is no recommendation to perform a CYP based DDI study in humans.

L1183: **Reviewer's Comments:** Per the FDA in vitro DDI guidance, "in addition to the combination of inhibition and induction, one should separately use inhibition mechanisms (A and B only) to predict a drug's enzyme inhibition potential (i.e., assuming C is equal to 1), and use induction mechanisms (C only) to predict a drug's enzyme induction potential (i.e., assuming A and B are equal to 1). It should be noted that concurrent prediction may lead to a false negative prediction if the inhibition potential is over-predicted, thus masking the induction effect. If the induction potential is over-predicted, it will mask the inhibition effect."
L1184: In the study report VMT-CYP-001, the Applicant conducted a mechanistic static assessment of the need to conduct a clinical DDI study using only CYP inhibition data. The analysis results indicate that the risk of a significant CYP inhibition based DDI is low for all the isoforms evaluated except CYP3A4/5. The Applicant did not conduct induction mechanistic static assessment by using CYP induction data only. Instead, after CYP inhibition mechanism assessment, the Applicant directly combined induction parameters in the inhibition assessment and concluded that a lack of significant AUCR values across all isoforms. Without a prediction of induction effect only, concurrent prediction may mask the induction effect if the inhibition potential is over-predicted. However, the Applicant's assessment is considered acceptable based on following reasons.
L1185: - When only CYP inhibition data were considered in the mechanistic static model analysis, the only isoform that was indicated as being potentially at risk for a clinically relevant drug interaction was CYP3A. Therefore, besides CYP3A inhibition, it is unlikely that inhibition of the other CYP isoforms are over-predicted.
- With regards to CYP3A4, a clinical drug interaction study for CYP3A4 was conducted by the Applicant using midazolam as the substrate. The study results demonstrated that oteseconazole is a weak inducer of CYP3A4. For further information, please see the clinical DDI section in this review.
L1186: At the End of Phase II Meeting the FDA raised the following concern: "Based on in vitro studies, there is a potential for DDI [drug-drug interactions] between VT-1161 and CYP2C8 substrates. Therefore, we recommend that you exclude subjects taking sensitive CYP2C8 substrates (e.g., repaglinide) from the trials." The FDA also stated the mechanistic static model requires assumptions that may not always be accurate and requested Viamet to provide clarification about the assumptions used in mechanistic static model calculations. To address the concern raised by the FDA, updated mechanistic static calculations for CYP2C8 were performed in study report VMT-CYP-003. The results of the updated mechanistic static model calculations
L1187: showed that calculated AUCR values did not fall outside of the 0.8 to 1.25 window for any CYP2C8 model substrate evaluated under any of the updated scenarios. The four scenarios assessed by the Applicant are as follows: Scenario 1 represents the updated base-case scenario for oteseconazole in the treatment of RVVC (150-mg dose). Scenario 2 represents lower protein binding than the base-case scenario. Scenario 3 represents higher [I]_{\max,b} values than the base-case scenario. Scenario 4 represents a worst-case scenario with a combination of lower protein binding and higher [I]_{\max,b} values than the base-case scenario.
L1188: ## Transporters
L1189: Bidirectional permeability studies were conducted by the Applicant to evaluate whether oteseconazole is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP. The study results showed that in the presence of bovine serum albumin (BSA), the efflux ratio of oteseconazole was below 2 in both the presence and absence of the P-gp inhibitor, valspodar, suggesting that oteseconazole is not a substrate of P-gp. The efflux ratio of oteseconazole in MDCKII-BCRP cells was below 2 in the presence and absence of a BCRP inhibitor, Ko143, suggesting that oteseconazole is not a substrate of BCRP.
L1190: To determine if oteseconazole is a substrate of human uptake transporters such as organic anion transporting polypeptides (OATP)1B1, OATP1B3, organic cation transporter (OCT)2, OAT1 and OAT3, the accumulation of oteseconazole in transporter-expressing and control HEK293 cells was measured. The accumulation of oteseconazole in OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT2, OAT1 and OAT3 expressing cells was not reduced in the presence of positive control inhibitors. The results suggest that oteseconazole is not a substrate of OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT2, OAT1 and OAT3.
L1191: In vitro inhibition study results showed that oteseconazole inhibited P-gp with calculated IC50 values of 2.88 µM and inhibited BCRP with IC50 values of 0.415 µM, respectively. Oteseconazole inhibited OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 with calculated IC50 values of 2.97 and 2.57 µM, respectively. Oteseconazole (2.5 and 25 µM) did not inhibit OCT2, OAT1 or OAT3.
L1192: **Reviewer's Comments:** In the clinical pharmacology summary, the Applicant mentioned that "the clinical relevance of in vitro inhibition of human transporters P-gp and BCRP was evaluated based on the analyses recommended by the FDA in the study report of VMT-CYP-002. The systemic efflux transporter assessment using the unbound Cmax for oteseconazole suggested that there was a risk of clinically relevant inhibition of BCRP, but not P-gp or OATP1B1/1B3". However, the study report VMT-CYP-002 was not submitted by the Applicant. Based on the FDA's In Vitro DDI guidance, R=1+((f_{u,p} \times I_{in,max})/IC_{50}) with cutoff value of 1.1 is used to determine whether the investigational drug has the potential to inhibit OATP1B1/3. The reviewer calculated R values (based on f_{u,p} = 0.5\%, I_{in,max} = 10\mu M, and MW=527.39 g/mol), for OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 are 1.03 and 1.04, respectively, which is less than the cutoff value of 1.1. Therefore, the results are in line with the Applicant's assessment. The oteseconazole concentration of 10\mu M used for [I] is the concentration used in the in vitro transporter inhibition study, which is higher than mean Cmax shown in the clinical studies and presents the worst-case scenario.
L1193: In addition, the Applicant has conducted clinical DDI studies with P-gp and BCRP substrates (Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-015). For detailed information, please see the clinical DDI section in this review.
L1194: ## Summary
L1195: Given that the Applicant did not conduct any in vitro studies to evaluate whether oteseconazole is a substrate or an inhibitor of (multidrug and toxic compound extrusion) MATE transporter in the original submission, an information request (IR) request was sent to the Applicant for clarification on 08/04/2021. However, the clarifications provided in the IR response dated 08/12/2021 were inadequate. Based on subsequent communication with the Applicant, the Applicant agreed to conduct in vitro MATE substrate and inhibitor interaction study in the post-marketing setting, if the NDA is approved.
L1196: ## 14.2. In Vivo Studies
L1197: ## 14.2.1. In Vivo Activity in an Estrogen-Dependent Murine Model of Vaginal Candidiasis
L1198: Study VMT-LSU-1 evaluated the antifungal activity of 4, 10, and 25 mg/kg doses of oteseconazole in mice vaginally infected with *C. albicans*. To maintain a persistent vaginal *candida* infection, female CBA/J mice (N=10/treatment group) were administered 0.1 mg SC estradiol 3 days prior to and 4 days following vaginal inoculation with a wild-type strain of *C. albicans*. Vehicle treated mice served as a negative control while mice treated with a 25 mg/kg dose of fluconazole served as the positive control. Beginning 3 days post-inoculation, treatments were administered once daily via oral gavage for 3 days. At the end of treatment, the vaginal cavity lavage fluid was collected on Study Day 7 (i.e., 1 day after the end of treatment) and Day 10 for enumeration of vaginal fungal burden. Blood and vaginal tissue were also collected on Study Day 10 for assessment of plasma and vaginal tissue drug concentrations. Drug concentrations were quantified using a validated LC-MS/MS method.
L1199: Compared to vehicle-treated mice, oteseconazole-treated mice exhibited a statistically significantly lower fungal burden at both Days 7 and 10 (p value \le0.0002). There was no apparent dose-response at Day 7 as all dose levels achieved median reductions in fungal burden of &gt;90% compared to vehicle. However, at Day 10, regrowth was observed at all but the 25 mg/kg dose level. Additionally, the 25 mg/kg dose of oteseconazole maintained the same degree of antifungal activity as the 25 mg/kg fluconazole dose at both Days 7 and 10 (Figure 7). Plasma and vaginal tissue drug concentrations were generally comparable, as summarized in Table 119.

L1205: Study VMT-LSU-2 was conducted as a follow-up to Study VMT-LSU-1 to evaluate the antifungal activity of 25 mg/kg dose of oteseconazole against a panel of clinical isolates resistant to fluconazole in the murine model of vaginal candidiasis. Similar to the preceding study, female CBA/J mice were rendered pseudo-estrus prior to vaginal inoculation, and treatment was initiated 3 days post-inoculation through Study Day 6. Vaginal lavage fluid and blood were collected as described for Study VMT-LSU-1 for quantitation of fungal burden and drug concentrations. Following initial feasibility studies, clinical isolates (fluconazole and oteseconazole MICs summarized in Table 120) were selected to evaluate the in vivo activity of oteseconazole. For most comparisons, oteseconazole was found to be statistically superior to vehicle and fluconazole in reducing fungal burden for isolates AR466-06 and JJ300-06. Results of a repeat experiment for isolate AR466-06 (Figure 8, left) were consistent with the original observations. For isolate LP1158-07, which had a higher oteseconazole minimum inhibitory concentration (MIC), the activity of oteseconazole was diminished, and appeared comparable to vehicle and fluconazole. Statistical analysis of the median CFU data is summarized in Table 121 for all 3 isolates. For isolate AR466-06, statistical analysis is shown for the first experiment only.

Consistent with observations from the nonclinical studies, there was a general trend towards reduced mycologic eradication with increasing MIC for the Phase 3 *C. albicans* isolates.

L1219: ## 14.2.1.1. Human PK Studies in Healthy Volunteers
L1220: ## Single Ascending Dose
L1221: Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-001 was a single ascending dose study to characterize the PK, safety and tolerability of oteseconazole in 64 healthy adult subjects. This study also featured an exploratory objective to evaluate the effect of high-fat food on the bioavailability of oteseconazole. Subjects were enrolled sequentially to 6 cohorts with 8 subjects per cohort, and randomly allocated 3:1 to receive either oteseconazole (at doses of 5, 10, 20, 40, 80 or 160 mg) or placebo under fasted conditions (defined as an overnight fast of at least 10 h with continued fasting for 4 h post-dosing). Food effect was evaluated in two additional cohorts (7 and 8), where subjects received 320 mg oteseconazole under fasted conditions or 30 min after a high-fat meal (defined as ~900 kcal total; 50% fat), respectively.
L1222: Blood sampling for PK assessment was conducted pre-dose and at pre-specified times through 72 h post-dosing during a 4-day confinement period. Thereafter, outpatient PK blood samples were obtained on Days 7 and 14 for Cohorts 1-6, and on Days 28 and 42 for Cohorts 7 and 8. Plasma PK samples were analyzed using a validated LC-MS/MS method. Pharmacokinetic assessments included: C_{max}, time to maximum concentration (T_{max}), the area under the curve from zero to infinity (AUC_{0-\infty}), apparent volume of distribution during terminal phase (V_z/F), apparent clearance (CL/F), and half-life (t_{1/2}).
L1223: Sixty of the sixty-four enrolled subjects completed the study. Oteseconazole was well tolerated and no study discontinuations were attributed to treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs); all
L1224: 4 subjects who failed to complete the study were lost to follow-up. The PK population was comprised of subjects with a sufficient number of concentration-time data to provide a PK profile for noncompartmental analysis. Because most of the plasma oteseconazole concentrations were below the lower limit of quantification (LLOQ) of 20.0 ng/mL for subjects who received a dose of 5 mg or 10 mg, data from these cohorts were excluded from the PK summary statistics presented in Table 122. Plasma concentration-time profiles for dose levels of 20 – 320 mg are shown in Figure 10.

L1239: ## Multiple Ascending Dose
L1240: Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-002 was a multiple ascending dose study to evaluate the PK, safety and tolerability of oteseconazole in 32 healthy adult subjects. Subjects were enrolled into 4 dosage groups of 40, 80, 160 and 320 mg in which they were randomized 3:1 to receive oteseconazole or placebo, respectively. Following an overnight fast of at least 10 hours, doses were administered 30 minutes after a standardized high-fat meal (900 kcal with 50% of caloric content from fat). Blood samples were collected pre-dose and at pre-specified times post-dosing for the 7 consecutive days of daily dosing. Subjects returned on Days 14, 21, 35, 28 and 49 for additional safety, PK and laboratory assessments. PK samples were assayed using a validated LC-MS/MS method. Pharmacokinetic assessments included: C_{max}, minimum plasma concentration (C_{min}), T_{max}, area under the curve to the last quantifiable time point (AUC_{0-\tau}), V_z/F, CL/F, accumulation ratio (R_{acc}), and t_{1/2}. C_{max}, C_{min}, T_{max}, area under the concentration-time curve during the dosing interval (AUC_\tau), and t_{1/2} were estimated by noncompartmental analysis. Additionally, a two-compartment model with first order absorption and a lag-time for absorption was used in compartmental analysis to improve estimates for CL/F and t_{1/2}.
L1241: Oteseconazole was well-tolerated and all but one subject (enrolled in the 80 mg cohort) failed to complete the study. This was due to the only adverse event (AE) (constipation) reported in this study and deemed unrelated to study drug. Consistent with its long half-life, significant accumulation was observed following repeat administration of oteseconazole, with the greatest accumulation observed at the highest dose (Table 124). Both C_{max} and AUC_\tau increased in an approximately dose proportional manner over the 40 to 320-mg dose range. Generally, V_z/F increased with increasing dose; CL/F decreased between the 40 and 80-mg dose levels but remained similar between the 80 to 320-mg dose levels. Mean concentration-time profiles at each dose level are provided in Figure 11.

L1253: ## VMT-VT-1161-CL-008: Multiple Dose and PK in Healthy Western Versus Japanese Subjects
L1254: Study CL-008 was a Phase 1, open-label study to determine the safety, tolerability and PK of multiple doses of 300 mg and 600 mg oteseconazole in healthy Western and Japanese subjects. A total of 51 subjects, 25 Japanese and 26 Western healthy volunteers were randomized to receive either 300 mg or 600 mg. Doses were administered at the clinical study site 30 min after the start of breakfast once daily for 14 days. Blood samples were collected pre-dose and at Days 2-12, Day 14-17 and at the Day 28 follow-up visit. Subjects returned on Days 14, 21, 35, 28 and 49 for additional safety, PK and laboratory assessments. PK samples were assayed using a validated LC-MS/MS method. Pharmacokinetic assessments included: Cmax, Cmin, Tmax, area under the curve from 0 to 72 hours (AUC0-72h) (for the Day 14 dose), Vz/F, CL/F and t1/2, which were summarized using noncompartmental analysis.
L1255: As with previous studies, oteseconazole was well tolerated. In the 300-mg dose group, 11 out of 13 Japanese and 12 out of 14 Western subjects completed the study. One Japanese subject discontinued the study due to AEs; other discontinuations were due to physician decision, protocol violation and withdrawal by subject. Similarly, in the 600-mg dose group, 10 out of 12 Japanese subjects and all 12 Western subjects completed the study. Discontinuations at the
L1256: higher dose level were due to protocol violation and withdrawal by subject. Table 126 summarizes PK parameters by dose level for Japanese and Western subjects.
L1257: **Reviewer's Comment:** At the 300-mg dose level, C_{max} and AUC_{0-72h} were ~32% and 20% higher, respectively, in Japanese subjects. At the 600-mg dose level, these differences in C_{max} and AUC_{0-72h} were ~17% and 5%, respectively. The reason underlying the small difference in exposure at the 300-mg dose level may be due to differences in body weight, which was shown to be a covariate for oteseconazole exposure in the population PK model. In the 300 mg cohorts, mean body weight for Japanese subjects was 60.25 kg, compared to 76.46 kg in Western subjects (i.e., ~27% lower body weight in Japanese subjects). In the 600 mg cohorts, this difference was less pronounced (67.29 kg versus 71.27 kg). Given the lack of difference in the safety and tolerability of oteseconazole between populations, these differences in exposure are not expected to be clinically significant.

L1262: ## Food Effect
L1263: Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-013 was a Phase 1, open-label study intended to assess the effect of high-fat, high-calorie and low-fat, low-calorie meals on the bioavailability of oteseconazole. This study was also intended to bridge the (b) (4) tablet formulation used in Phase 1 extrinsic factor studies and Phase 2b studies to the Phase 3 capsule formulation. To meet these goals, 44 healthy adult females were allocated to 1 of 4 cohorts as follows:
L1264: - Fasted cohort (N=12): received a single 150 mg capsule on following an overnight fast of at least 10 h
- High-fat, high-calorie fed cohort (N=12): received a 150 mg capsule 30 minutes after the start of a high-fat, high-calorie meal (900-1000 kcal with 500-600 kcal from fat)
- Low-fat, low-calorie fed cohort (N=12): received a 150 mg capsule 30 minutes after the start of a low-fat, low-calorie meal (650 kcal with 117 kcal from fat)
- Comparative bioavailability cohort (N=8): received a (b) (4) 150-mg tablet 30 minutes after the start of a high-fat, high-calorie meal.
L1265: **Reviewer's Comment:** Although the total caloric content of the low-fat, low-calorie meal exceeds the 400-500 kcal recommended by the FDA guidance for industry titled, "Assessing the Effects of Food on Drugs in INDs and NDAs" (February 2019), the total number of kcal from fat falls within the recommended 100-125 kcal range for such as meal.
L1266: PK samples were collected pre-dose and through 72 h post-dose and analyzed using a validated LC-MS/MS method for assessment of the following PK parameters: Cmax, Tmax, AUC0-∞, AUC0-72h, VZ/F, CL/F, and t1/2. To assess the impact of food on oteseconazole exposure, an analysis of variance (ANOVA) was conducted using cohort designation as fixed effect. Least-square means of Cmax and AUC0-72h were estimated to obtain the geometric mean ratios (GMRs) for the fed versus fasted cohorts.
L1267: With the exception of 1 subject (in the low-fat, low-calorie fed cohort) who experienced vomiting 4 h post-dosing, all other subjects completed the study and were included in the PK analysis. High-fat, high-calorie food was found to increase the Cmax and AUC0-72h by 45% and 36%, respectively as summarized in Table 127. However, following administration of a low-fat, low-calorie meal, Cmax and AUC0-72h increased by only 17% and 2%, respectively (Table 128). Under fed conditions, Cmax and AUC0-72h associated with the 150 mg (b) (4) tablet was comparable to that of the Phase 3 150 mg capsule formulation as shown in Table 129.

L1277: ## Mass Balance
L1278: ## Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-016
L1279: Study CL-016 evaluated the mass balance, safety and tolerability of oteseconazole in 10 healthy adult female subjects of nonchildbearing potential, following administration of a single 600 mg oral dose of [^{14}\text{C}]oteseconazole containing 5 \mu\text{Ci} of radiocarbon with a high-fat, high-calorie meal. Subjects remained at the clinical study site for 17 days post-dosing for collection of blood and excreta samples, returning for 5 in-patient visits at prespecified timepoints through Study Day 279 (approximately 10 months post-dosing).
L1280: In anticipation of variability daily fecal excretion volume and contents, in-patient follow-up visits spanned 3 days to provide 48 consecutive hours of continuous fecal collection. 24 h radiolabel fecal excretion was then determined as half of the total 48 h recovery during the confinement period. Recovery of radioactivity in urine and feces was interpolated between scheduled study visits. For urine, the following equation was used:
L1281: \frac{\%d + \%f}{2} \times (tf - 1) - td + \%f, \text{ where:}
L1282: %d = radioactivity in % collected from the last collection interval prior to follow-up
L1283: %f = radioactivity in % collected from the follow-up visit sample
L1284: tf = study day of the follow-up visit
L1285: td = study day of the last collection interval prior to follow-up
L1286: For feces, a modified equation was implemented, correcting for variable feces voiding as follows:
L1287: \frac{Cd + Cf}{24h} \times \left[ \frac{\Sigma Wh}{408h} \times (hd - hf) \right], \text{ where:}
L1288: Cd = concentration in feces sample collected from the last collection interval prior to follow-up (dpm/g)
L1289: Cf = concentration in feces sample collected from the follow-up visit (dpm/g)
L1290: \Sigma Wh = estimated weight of feces produced between collections (g)
L1291: 24 h = hours in a day; 408 h = hours during initial confinement
L1292: hf = hour at end of collection period prior to follow-up (h)
L1293: hd = hour at start of collection period of the follow-up visit (h)
L1294: ## *Analytical Methods*
L1295: Quantitation of oteseconazole in plasma was performed using a validated LC-MS/MS method with an LLOQ of 20.0 ng/mL and upper limit of quantification of 40,000 ng/mL. Radioanalysis of urine and fecal homogenate samples was conducted using liquid scintillation counting; urine samples were added directly to a liquid scintillant and feces homogenate was combusted in a sample oxidizer for analysis. Samples were counted for at least 10 min or until 2\%2\sigma terminator was reached. For liquid scintillation counting, LLOQ was defined as twice the background count rate, and samples below LLOQ were analyzed by accelerator mass spectrometry. Plasma and
L1296: blood samples were also analyzed by accelerator mass spectrometry and the LLOQ was defined as 15% above the percentage Modern Carbon (i.e., pMC which equals 0.01356 dpm/mg) value in pre-dose samples of the same source and matrix. Plasma samples collected following the initial confinement underwent metabolite profiling via high-performance liquid chromatography followed by accelerator mass spectrometry analysis.
L1297: ## Results
L1298: The median age of the 10 subjects enrolled in this study was 55.5 (range: 46 – 61) years. Of these, 8 subjects completed the study. PK data from subjects who withdrew from the study were included in average statistics until the time of their discontinuation from the study. Within the first 408 h, the mean cumulative recovery of radioactivity was only 8.92%, 6.32% of which was recovered in feces and 2.29% in urine as summarized in Table 130. At the termination of the study (Day 278), the mean total recovered radioactivity was &lt;90%, i.e., 81.6 ± 9.68% (range: 64.1 - 95.3%) due to oteseconazole's exceptionally long half-life. Feces accounted for 56.0 ± 12.3% (range: 30.9 - 70.1%) of the recovered radioactivity while urine accounted for 25.6 ± 10.3% (range: 12.1 - 33.2%).

L1305: **Reviewer's Comment:** Of note, the Applicant's rat ADME study evaluated mass balance in both bile duct cannulated and intact male rats orally administered [^{14}\text{C}]-oteseconazole containing
L1306: 200 \mu Ci/kg. In this study, mean % recovery of total radioactivity was 62.5% after 120 h in cannulated rats and 85.7% through 504 h post-dosing in intact rats. Interestingly, the % recovery of radioactivity in feces through 120 h post-dosing was similar in cannulated and intact rats (i.e., 42% versus 47%, respectively). Approximately 26-29% of total radioactivity was recovered in feces within the first 24 h in the two rat cohorts, which was attributed to unabsorbed drug or intestinal secretion. Only 20% of the total radioactivity was recovered in bile, with 0.7% of the administered dose recovered in feces.
L1307: In humans, however, only 6.32% of total radioactivity was recovered in feces within the first 408 h post-dosing, which the Applicant assumes to be unabsorbed drug. In vitro, oteseconazole was not a substrate of the P-gp and BCRP intestinal efflux transporters. However, the Applicant did not determine if oteseconazole is a substrate of MRP2, another intestinal transporter. Therefore, the contribution of intestinal secretion to the fecal recovery of radioactivity cannot be ruled out.
L1308: In addition to being expressed on the intestinal membrane, MRP2 is also expressed on the bile canalicular membrane along with MATE and BSEP. Because the Applicant did not determine if oteseconazole is a substrate of any of these transporters, their involvement in the disposition of oteseconazole cannot be ruled out. The majority of fecal recovery, which occurs after the initial 408 h confinement period, suggests the involvement of mechanisms other than excretion of unabsorbed oteseconazole and/or intestinal secretion. Based on the observed biliary recovery in the rat ADME study, biliary secretion of oteseconazole is also likely in humans. Therefore, the prolonged recovery of oteseconazole in feces may be primarily due to biliary secretion mediated by MRP2, MATE or BSEP efflux transporters.
L1309: All subjects who completed the study had quantifiable levels of plasma and whole blood radioactivity through the last sampling time of 6634 h post-dosing. The blood-to-plasma ratio of total radioactivity approximates 1, indicating that oteseconazole is equally distributed between blood cells and plasma (Table 131). Plasma total radioactivity and plasma oteseconazole exposures were comparable, as indicated by the respective AUC values summarized in Table 131. This observation, in addition to the finding that unchanged oteseconazole accounted for 97.1% of circulating plasma radioactivity in 0-408 h pooled plasma samples obtained for metabolite profiling, indicates that oteseconazole does not undergo significant metabolism. Consequently, urine and fecal samples were not analyzed for possible metabolites. The estimated terminal half-life of oteseconazole was ~2114 h (~88 days) and similar to the terminal half-life of radioactivity in plasma and whole blood (Table 131).

L1317: **Reviewer's Comment:** In the proposed labeling, the Applicant states that oteseconazole has an oral bioavailability of 94% based on mass balance study results. In response to an IR (dated July 21st 2021) which sought clarification on the Applicant's approach to determining oral bioavailability from mass balance study results, the Applicant offered the following explanation:
L1318: - Based on the expectation that elimination of oteseconazole continues after study termination on Day 278 (at which point cumulative 81.6% of radioactivity had been recovered), the Applicant anticipates that recovery would eventually reach 100%.
- The 6.32% of radioactivity excreted via feces during the first 408 h collection interval is exclusively nonabsorbed oteseconazole.
- Using the assumption that recovery would eventually reach 100% and that 6.32% of oteseconazole was excreted unabsorbed, the oral bioavailability of oteseconazole was estimated to be 94%.
L1319: However, the final sampling timepoints suggest that recovery of radioactivity has plateaued around 81.6%. Thus, it's unclear that continued collection of excreta would culminate in 100% recovery of the radiolabeled dose. Therefore, it would be more appropriate to assume 81.6% cumulative recovery in estimating bioavailability. If 6.32% of oteseconazole is excreted as unabsorbed drug, and 81.6% of total radioactivity is recovered, the estimated oral bioavailability of oteseconazole is 75.3%.
L1320: ## Effect of Oteseconazole on the QTc Interval
L1321: The effect of oteseconazole on the QTc interval was evaluated in Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-018, a double-blind, randomized, multiple-dose, placebo- and positive-controlled, crossover study in 59 healthy female volunteers. Subjects were randomized to oteseconazole, moxifloxacin (positive control)/placebo or placebo/moxifloxacin in a 2:1:1 ratio. In the oteseconazole arm, 600 mg of oteseconazole was administered daily on Days 1-13 followed by a 1200-mg dose on Day 14. For the moxifloxacin/placebo arm, a single dose of 400 mg moxifloxacin was
L1322: administered on Day 1 followed by daily administration of placebo through Day 14. Finally, the placebo/moxifloxacin arm was administered placebo on Days 1-14 and a single dose of 400 mg moxifloxacin on Day 15. Blood samples and continuous electrocardiogram measurements were obtained at pre-specified timepoints pre-dose through Day 17 post-dose for the oteseconazole and the combined moxifloxacin/placebo arms. No significant QTc interval prolongation was detected for oteseconazole. The upper bound of the two-sided 90% CI for the mean change in QTc was -10.5 msec (please see the QT Study Review authored by the Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac Safety Studies uploaded to DARRTS on December 8th, 2021).
L1323: ## Summary of Clinical Drug-Drug Interaction Studies
L1324: ## Evaluation of Inhibition/Induction Effect of Oteseconazole on Sensitive CYP3A4 Substrate (Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-007)
L1325: Oteseconazole was identified as a CYP3A4 inhibitor and inducer in vitro. Thus, the Applicant conducted a clinical DDI study to evaluate the inhibition and induction effect of oteseconazole on a prototypical sensitive CYP3A4 substrate, midazolam. Subjects were enrolled in a fixed sequence design to receive a single 2 mg oral dose of midazolam on Days 1, 3, and 16. Subjects received 14 daily 600 mg oral doses of oteseconazole (four 150-mg tablets) on Day 3 through Day 16. Midazolam and oteseconazole were administered in the morning 30 minutes after a high-fat, high-calorie meal. A validated LC-MS/MS method was used for quantification of oteseconazole and midazolam concentrations in plasma. No serious TEAEs were observed in the study.
L1326: The study results suggest that coadministration of oteseconazole did not result in clinically significant inhibition of midazolam metabolism and caused weak induction of CYP3A4 with a 36% decrease in AUC of midazolam on day 16 compared to AUC on day 1. Note that the dosage regimen used for oteseconazole in this study is 600 mg once daily for 14 consecutive days, but the proposed maintenance dose of oteseconazole is 150 mg weekly. The C_{max} of oteseconazole on Day 16 is approximately 3-fold higher than the therapeutic exposure for the targeted indications seen in the Phase 3 studies. Therefore, it is unlikely there is a significant DDI between oteseconazole and CYP3A4 substrates at the proposed maintenance dose of oteseconazole, and thus no dosage adjustment is needed for drugs that are CYP3A4 substrates when co-administered with oteseconazole.

L1333: ## **Evaluation of Inhibition Effect of Oteseconazole on P-gp and BCRP Substrates (Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-015)**
L1334: Oteseconazole was identified as a potential BCRP inhibitor in vitro, therefore, the Applicant conducted a clinical DDI study with rosuvastatin, a probe substrate for the BCRP transporter to evaluate inhibition effect of oteseconazole on BCRP membrane transporters. Given the common clinical assessments of interactions with digoxin as a substrate for P-gp, this study was also performed to rule out potential clinically relevant interactions with this transporter. This was a Phase 1, single-center, open-label, DDI study in healthy adult subjects to evaluate the effects of multiple oral doses of oteseconazole on the PK of a single oral dose of digoxin, a probe substrate for the P-gp transporter, and a single dose of rosuvastatin. Subjects received 0.5 mg digoxin or rosuvastatin 20 mg on Days 1, 3, and 16 and 150 mg oteseconazole once daily (QD) on Days 3 through 16. All doses were administered in the morning 30 minutes after ingesting a high-fat, high-calorie meal. In this study, three different validated analytical methods (LC-MS/MS) were used for quantification of the plasma concentrations of digoxin, rosuvastatin, and oteseconazole.
L1335: ## *Digoxin Cohort*
L1336: The study results showed that Cmax and area under the curve from 0 to 24 hours (AUC0-24h) of digoxin were similar (differences in mean ratios were approximately 6 - 13%) following the coadministration of a single dose of 0.5 mg digoxin and multiple doses of 150 mg oteseconazole (Day 16) compared with digoxin alone (Day 1). However, AUC0-∞ of digoxin was increased by around 52% after single dose of oteseconazole and was decreased by around 40% after multiple doses of oteseconazole. It is to be noted that out of 47 subjects who completed the study, AUC0-∞ of digoxin was reported from only 9 subjects on day 1, 3 subjects on day 3 and 14 subjects on day 16. Per the study report, *the λz values could not be assigned for subjects if the R2 value was*
L1337: &lt; 0.75, or the terminal elimination phase was not evident, and therefore, values for \lambda_z and its associated parameters, (i.e., t_{1/2}, AUC_{0-\infty}, CL/F, and Vz/F) were considered not calculable and were not reported. These are presented as missing in the PK parameter tables. As around 81% of subjects on day 1 and around 94% of subjects on day 3 were excluded for AUC_{0-\infty} assessment, AUC_{0-\infty} of digoxin is not a reliable PK parameter for DDI assessment in this study. As digoxin is a long half-life drug (t_{1/2}: 1.5-2 days), it is unknown whether AUC_{0-24h} of digoxin is a reliable PK parameter to evaluate the DDI study. A literature search was conducted to find out whether P-gp inhibitor can have an inhibition effect on digoxin within 24 hours. The results showed that the inhibition effect on digoxin by a P-gp inhibitor can be observed within 24 hours (e.g., capmatinib (Grande et al. 2021) and fostamatinib (Martin et al. 2015). Therefore, C_{max} and AUC_{0-24h} of digoxin were used for DDI assessment in this study. The study results suggest that the C_{max} and AUC_{0-24h} of digoxin were not affected in the presence of single or multiple doses of oteseconazole. Note that the current study was conducted under the worst-case scenario as oteseconazole was given 150 mg daily for 14 days. Therefore, it is unlikely there is a significant DDI between oteseconazole and P-gp substrates at the proposed maintenance dose of oteseconazole, and thus no dosage adjustment is needed for drugs that are P-gp substrates when co-administered with oteseconazole.

L1348: ## *Rosuvastatin Cohort*
L1349: The statistical comparisons of plasma rosuvastatin PK parameters in the presence and absence of oteseconazole are summarized in Table 134. Based on the geometric mean ratios, mean C_{max}, AUC_{0-24h} and AUC_{0-\infty}, were approximately 84% to 87% higher following coadministration of a single dose of 20 mg rosuvastatin and a single dose of 150 mg oteseconazole (Day 3) compared with the administration of rosuvastatin alone (Day 1). Based on the geometric mean ratios, mean C_{max} and AUC of rosuvastatin, were approximately 94% to 118% higher following the coadministration of a single dose of 20 mg rosuvastatin and multiples doses of 150 mg oteseconazole (Day 16) compared with the administration of rosuvastatin alone (Day 1). Therefore, it is concluded that oteseconazole is an inhibitor of BCRP.

L1357: **Reviewer's Comment:** Notably, the oteseconazole regimen used in this study (i.e., 150 mg daily for 14 days) yields Cmax and AUC24h values that are 30% and 16% higher than Cmax and AUC24h, respectively, at Week 13 for the recommended dosing regimen for RVVC. Thus, the results of this DDI study present the worst-case scenario for increases in rosuvastatin exposure when used concomitantly with oteseconazole.
L1358: ## **Evaluation of the Effect of Oteseconazole on Oral Contraceptive (OC) (VMT-VT-1161-CL-014)**
L1359: This was a Phase 1, single-center, open-label, DDI study in healthy adult females designed to evaluate the effects of multiple oral doses of oteseconazole tablets on the PK of single oral doses of an OC containing ethinyl estradiol (EE) and norethindrone (NE). A total of 24 subjects received a single oral dose of an OC containing a fixed-dose combination of 35 µg EE and 1 mg NE on Day 1 and Day 21 together with 150 mg oteseconazole on Days 8 through 21 in a fixed-sequence design. The objective of this study was to evaluate the effects of multiple oral doses of oteseconazole on the PK of a single oral dose of contraceptive drugs containing EE and NE.
L1360: As stipulated in the FDA draft guidance for industry entitled “*Clinical Drug Interaction Studies With Combined Oral Contraceptives*” (November 2020), an approximately 40 percent increase in EE concentration for combined oral contraceptives (COC) containing 35 µg EE resulting in exposures similar to COC containing 50 µg EE could be clinically meaningful. Based on the
L1361: GMRs, mean peak and extent of exposure to EE, as measured by mean C_{max}, AUC_{0-24h}, AUC_{0-t}, and AUC_{0-\infty}, were approximately 12% to 20% higher following multiple daily doses of oteseconazole. As the exposure increase is less than 40%, it is unlikely the proposed dosage regimen of oteseconazole would have a significant clinical impact on the EE's exposure.

L1369: ## Phase 2 Studies
L1370: ## Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-004
L1371: Study CL-004 was a Phase 2a, randomized, double-blind, dose-ranging study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of oteseconazole in comparison to a single 150-mg dose of fluconazole in the treatment of subjects with moderate to severe acute vulvovaginal candidiasis. Subjects were initially randomized 3:3:2 to oteseconazole dosing regimens of 300 mg QD or 600 mg QD for 3 days, or a single dose of fluconazole (plus placebo to maintain blinding). Following an interim analysis of safety, efficacy and PK, a third oteseconazole dose level (600 mg twice daily [BID] for 3 days) was added to the study, and subjects were enrolled in a 3:1 ratio to the high dose oteseconazole arm and fluconazole, respectively. The primary efficacy objective was to evaluate the composite of clinical and mycological efficacy of the 3 oteseconazole dose levels compared to fluconazole at the test of cure (TOC) i.e., Day 28 visit. Secondary objectives included:
L1372: - Evaluation of the safety and tolerability of oteseconazole at the 3 dose levels
- Evaluation of clinical efficacy alone at the TOC
- Evaluation of mycological efficacy alone at Days 7, 14 and TOC
- Evaluation of PK of the 3 oteseconazole dose levels
L1373: Oteseconazole dose selection for this study was informed by the safety and PK of oteseconazole in SAD and MAD studies as well as findings from nonclinical studies in a murine model of vaginal candidiasis. Results from the murine studies indicated that plasma oteseconazole concentrations \ge 1 \mu\text{g/mL} were associated with &gt;90% median reduction in fungal burden. Based on the exposures observed in Phase 1 SAD and MAD studies, the 3 oteseconazole dosing regimens were predicted through modeling and simulation to yield Cmin values in excess of the 1 \mu\text{g/mL} threshold.
L1374: Subjects were instructed to take study drug 30 minutes after consuming a meal, however the types and caloric contents of meals were not specified. Sparse PK sampling was conducted beginning at baseline through 168 days post-dosing and analyzed using a validated LC-MS/MS method. Additionally, samples were obtained from consenting subjects on Study Day 1 at pre-dose and 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6 and 8 h post-dosing. The oteseconazole plasma PK parameters of interest in this study were: C_{max}, C_{min}, T_{max}, area under the curve from 0 to 8 hours (AUC_{0-8h}) and t_{1/2}.
L1375: Of the 40 female subjects randomized to the oteseconazole treatment arms (where N = 12 to 15 subjects/treatment group), 35 subjects completed the study. Nineteen subjects treated with oteseconazole were reported to have TEAEs, none of which led to study discontinuation; five subjects who received oteseconazole were lost to follow-up. The primary efficacy endpoint of therapeutic cure was achieved in 64.3%, 75% and 78.6% of subjects in the 300 mg QD, 600 mg QD and 600 mg BID treatment arms, respectively, compared to 66.7% for fluconazole in the ITT population as summarized in **Table 137**. Statistically, there was no significant difference in cure rates for any of the oteseconazole arms compared to fluconazole. PK parameters following single dose administration of 300 mg or 600 mg are summarized in **Table 138**. Mean C_{min} values at 24 hours following the final dose were calculated using the estimated t_{1/2} obtained on Days 7 to 168 and determined to be 0.71 µg/mL, 1 µg/mL and 2.8 µg/mL for the 300 mg QD, 600 mg QD and 600 mg BID treatment arms, respectively.
L1376: Per the Applicant, the comparatively higher cure rates for the 600 mg QD and 600 mg BID arms compared to the 300 mg QD arm may be attributed to the lower oteseconazole exposures (particularly C_{min}) in the low dose group.

L1384: **Reviewer's Comment:** Numerically, the efficacy of the 300 mg QD regimen is comparable to that of the fluconazole arm, while the 600 mg treatment arms provide numerically higher efficacy rates. While the sample size is small, observations from the study support use of 600 mg QD as part of the loading dose/treatment of acute VVC.
L1385: ## *Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-006*
L1386: Study CL-006 was a Phase 2b, randomized, placebo-controlled dose-ranging study to evaluate the efficacy of 4 oteseconazole dosing regimens for the prevention of culture-verified acute VVC through Week 48 (of the study) in subjects with RVVC. Secondary objectives were to:
L1387: - Evaluate the proportion of subjects with culture-verified acute VVC through Week 24
- Evaluate the safety and tolerability of the oteseconazole dosing regimens through Week 48
- Evaluate the PK of the 4 dosing regimens.
L1388: The study was conducted in two phases: subjects (N=254) were first enrolled in an induction phase in which they received 3 doses of 150 mg fluconazole administered every 72 h for the treatment of the acute episode of VVC, followed by a maintenance phase in which they were randomized to 1 of 5 treatment arms. Subjects who were determined to have resolution of their acute VVC infection at 14 days post-initiation of fluconazole treatment were then randomized to the 5 maintenance phase treatment arms as follows:
L1389: - 150 mg oteseconazole daily for 1 week + 150 mg oteseconazole weekly for 11 weeks + placebo for 12 weeks (N=42)
- 150 mg oteseconazole daily for 1 week + 150 mg oteseconazole weekly for 23 weeks (N=43)
- 300 mg oteseconazole daily for 1 week + 300 mg oteseconazole weekly for 11 weeks + placebo for 12 weeks (N=43)
- 300 mg oteseconazole daily for 1 week + 300 mg oteseconazole weekly for 23 weeks (N=41)
- Placebo daily for 1 week + placebo weekly for 23 weeks (N=46)
L1390: Selection of the oteseconazole dosing regimens was informed by safety and PK data from Phase 1 SAD and MAD studies, preclinical murine efficacy studies, and the Phase 2a dose-ranging study in subjects with acute VVC. Based on the results of these preceding studies, dosing regimens anticipated to yield Cmin values \ge 1 \mug/mL were selected for evaluation. Subjects were instructed to take study drug 30 minutes after the main meal of the day at the same time of day throughout the study. Sparse PK sampling was conducted beginning at baseline through Week 48, and samples were analyzed using a validated LC-MS/MS method. Additionally, samples were obtained from consenting subjects on Study Day 7 at pre-dose and 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6 and 8 h post-dosing. For this study, the plasma PK parameters of interest were: Cmax, Cmin, Tmax and AUC0-8h (at Day 7).
L1391: A total of 39 subjects discontinued study participation prematurely, with the most commonly listed reason being loss to follow up (n=16). The number of subjects who completed the study was similar across treatment groups and there were no notable differences in the incidence of TEAEs across all treatment groups (including placebo). Two subjects (from the 150 mg and 300 mg 12-week treatment arms) experienced TEAEs leading to study discontinuation. For the primary endpoint, the percentage of subjects who experienced an acute VVC infection through Week 48 was lower in the oteseconazole treatment groups compared to placebo, and similar between oteseconazole treatment arms as summarized in Table 139 (please see Sections 6.2 and 7.6 for detailed discussion of the safety and efficacy of oteseconazole in this study). As would be expected, exposures for the 150 mg 12 week and 24-week regimens were comparable, as were the exposures for the 300 mg 12 week and 24-week regimens (Table 140).

L1402: **Reviewer's Comments:** Although exposures associated with the 300 mg regimens were ~2-fold what was observed with the 150 mg regimens, numerically, efficacy rates were comparable across oteseconazole treatment arms. Therefore, results from this study support further evaluation of the 150 mg 12-week regimen for the prevention of recurrence in Phase 3 trials.
L1403: ## **Phase 3 Studies**
L1404: ## *Studies VMT-VT-1161-CL-011 and VMT-VT-1161-CL-012*
L1405: Studies CL-011 and CL-012 were identically designed Phase 3, multicenter, multinational, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled studies. These studies were designed to evaluate the efficacy and safety or oral oteseconazole capsules in the treatment of recurrent vulvovaginal candidiasis in females aged 12 years and older. Both studies were conducted in 2 phases as follows:
L1406: - An open-label induction phase using 3 doses of 150 mg oral fluconazole administered every 72 h for the treatment of the acute VVC episode present at the time of screening
- A maintenance phase in which subjects were randomized 2:1 to receive either 150 mg oteseconazole or placebo daily for 7 days followed by weekly dosing for 11 weeks beginning 1 week after completion of daily dosing regimen.
L1407: Selection of the oteseconazole dosing regimens was informed by safety and PK data from Phase 1 SAD and MAD studies, preclinical murine efficacy studies, and the two Phase 2 dose-ranging studies conducted in subjects with acute and recurrent VVC. Given that the 150 mg 12-week regimen was as efficacious as the higher dose and longer duration regimens in the Phase 2b study, it was expected to be sufficient for the prevention of recurrence of VVC. The primary efficacy endpoint was defined as the proportion of subjects who experienced 1 or more episodes of culture-verified acute VVC (considered a recurrent episode) during the maintenance phase in the ITT population. The secondary endpoints included:
L1408: - Time to first recurrence of culture-verified acute VVC episode with signs and symptoms score \ge 3 during the maintenance phase
- Proportion of subjects with at least 1 positive culture for *Candida* during the maintenance phase
- Proportion of subjects with at least 1 culture-verified acute VVC episode with signs and symptoms score \ge 3 post-randomization through Week 24
L1409: To ensure compliance, subjects were instructed to ingest study medication within 30 minutes after their main meal of the day (as determined by the subject) at approximately the same time of day. Blood samples were obtained from a small cohort of Phase 3 subjects pre-dosing, at Day 14 and prior to weekly drug product administration on Weeks 12, 24, 36 and 48 post administration of the first dose of oteseconazole. Plasma samples were analyzed using a validated LC-MS/MS method.
L1410: ***Reviewer's Comment:*** *The type and caloric contents of foods administered with oteseconazole was not recorded. At the End-of-Phase 2 meeting discussions, the clinical pharmacology team agreed with the Applicant's plan to have oteseconazole administered with the day's largest meal (of the subject's choosing) as long as dosing instructions were clearly detailed in the study protocol.*
L1411: In Study CL-011, 217 subjects were randomized to receive oteseconazole and 109 subjects were randomized to the placebo arm. Similarly, 217 subjects were also randomized to the oteseconazole arm in Study CL-012 while 110 subjects were randomized to placebo. Oteseconazole was well tolerated in both studies; 182 subjects in study CL-011 and 191 subjects in Study CL-012 completed their respective studies and most early discontinuations were
L1412: attributed to subject withdrawal of consent (please see Sections 6.2 and 7.6 for detailed discussion of the safety and efficacy of oteseconazole in this study). Mean oteseconazole plasma concentrations observed in studies CL-011 and CL-012 were comparable and are summarized by study visit in Table 141.

L1416: ## *Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-017*
L1417: Study CL-017 was a Phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group study intended to evaluate the efficacy and safety of oteseconazole for the treatment of acute VVC in females with RVVC aged 12 years and older. As with the preceding studies CL-011 and CL-012, study CL-017 was conducted in two phases as follows:
L1418: - 2:1 randomization (N=219) to induction with either 600 mg oteseconazole on Day 1 and 450 mg on Day 2 or 3 doses of 150 mg fluconazole administered 72 h apart to treat the acute infection
- Beginning on Day 14, maintenance dosing with 150 mg oteseconazole or placebo (for the fluconazole induction arm) weekly for 11 weeks
L1419: Although the oteseconazole induction dosing regimen was not explicitly studied in previous Phase 2 studies, this regimen was expected to yield clinically effective oteseconazole plasma concentrations (i.e., \ge 1 \mug/mL based on previous Phase 2 studies) for the treatment of acute VVC. The primary endpoint was the proportion of subjects with 1 or more culture verified acute VVC episodes post-randomization through Week 50 in the ITT population. Secondary endpoints included:
L1420: - Proportion of subjects with at least 1 positive culture for *Candida* during the maintenance phase (post-Day 14 through Week 50)
- Proportion of subjects with at least 1 culture verified acute VVC episode with signs and symptoms score \ge 3 during the maintenance phase
L1421: As with studies CL-011 and CL-012, subjects were instructed to take study medication within 30 minutes after their main meal of the day. Blood samples for oteseconazole PK assessment were obtained at pre-dose and at Day 14, Week 14 (end of dosing) and Week 50 (end of follow-up period) post initiation of treatment. Additional PK samples were obtained from a small subset of consenting subjects on Days 1 and 2 (at 1, 2, 4 and 8 h post-dosing) to facilitate noncompartmental analysis. Plasma samples were analyzed using a validated LC-MS/MS method.
L1422: Of the 219 subjects entered into the induction phase, 147 were randomized to the oteseconazole group and of these, 35 total subjects discontinued the study prematurely largely due to induction failure (n=22). The proportion of subjects with 1 or more culture verified acute VVC episodes post-randomization through Week 50 in the ITT population was lower in the oteseconazole arm
L1423: (5.1%) compared to the fluconazole + placebo arm (42.2%); this difference was found to be statistically significant (please see Sections 6.2 and 7.6 for detailed discussion of the safety and efficacy of oteseconazole in this study). Oteseconazole plasma PK parameters associated with the induction dosing regimen are summarized in Table 142. Mean Day 14, Week 14 and Week 50 plasma concentrations were 1.40 µg/mL, 2.68 µg/mL and 0.87 µg/mL, respectively.

L1431: The Applicant explored the relationship between quartiles of Day 14 exposure from studies CL-011, CL-012 and CL-017 and recurrence of acute VVC in the safety and maintenance phase ITT population. To this end, the Applicant performed a logistic regression analysis on the proportion of subjects who experienced ≥ 1 episode of culture verified acute VVC during the maintenance phase and estimated the hazard ratio for the time to recurrence. The overall hazard ratio for time to recurrence was low across oteseconazole exposure quartiles, ranging from 0.03 to 0.17. Statistically, the proportion of subjects treated with oteseconazole (regardless of Day 14 exposure) who experienced a recurrence of VVC infection was lower than the proportion of subjects treated with fluconazole + placebo (designated as BLQ group) who experienced recurrence in the maintenance phase (Table 143).

L1435: ## 14.3. Pharmacometrics Review
L1436: In general, the Applicant's population PK analysis is considered acceptable in capturing oteseconazole's PK. More specifically, the developed model was used to support the current submission as outlined below:
L1437: **Table 144. Utility of the Population PK Model**
L1438: Utility of the final model	Reviewer's Comments
Derive exposure metrics and PK parametersThe popPK was used to assess the adequacy of the proposed fixed dosing in all patients from adolescents to adults.
The popPK model was used to predict oteseconazole maximum and minimum concentrations (C_{max} and C_{min}) and the area under the concentration-time curve over the dosing interval (AUC_T) after each dose, as well as predict oteseconazole half-life.
PK simulations shows that at lower body weights ranging from 29 to <40 kg (expected to be most prevalent in younger adolescent population < 16 years old), the median AUC_T, median C_{max}, and median C_{min} is about 2-fold the median values observed in patients with body weight ranging from 60 to 70 kg (Figure 15 and Figure 16).
The clinical studies did not include adolescent patients younger than 16 years old (3 patients with age 16 to 17 years old). No treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were observed in these patients. Only one patient weighing <40 kg was enrolled in the Phase 3 studies (VMT-VT-1161-CL-011, VMT-VT-1161-CL-012, VMT-VT-1161-CL-017), and patients weighing between 40 to 59 kg did not have increased AEs compared to those weighing at least 60 kg. Therefore, given the favorable safety profile of oteseconazole, no dose adjustment based on body weight is warranted in adolescent patients.
L1439: Source: Reviewer
Abbreviations: AEs, adverse events; AUC_T, area under the curve during a dosing interval; C_{max}, maximum plasma concentration; C_{min}, minimum plasma concentration; PopPK, population pharmacokinetics.
L1440: ## 14.3.1. Applicant's Population PK Analysis
L1441: The population pharmacokinetic (popPK) analysis was based on PK data from 8 studies (Table 145): four Phase 1 studies in healthy subjects and four Phase 2 studies in different patients' populations with interdigital tinea pedis (VMT-VT-1161-CL-003), toenail onychomycosis
L1442: NDA 215888
Vivjoa (oteseconazole)
L1443: (VMT-VT-1161-CL-005), and acute or recurrent vulvovaginal candidiasis (VMT-VT-1161-CL-004 and VMT-VT-1161-CL-006).
L1444: Oteseconazole (or VT-1161) was administered orally as single and multiple doses of 5 to 600 mg through 24 weeks (Table 145), either under fasted condition, with a high-fat breakfast or regular meal (Table 146), and either as capsules (VMT-VT-1161-CL-001 and VMT-VT-1161-CL-002), over-encapsulated tablets (VMT-VT-1161-CL-003 and VMT-VT-1161-CL-004) or tablets without over-encapsulation (Table 146).
L1445: The analysis dataset was comprised of 8033 PK observations from 604 subjects, including 496 BLQ (below limit of quantification: 20 ng/mL) concentrations. Among the 604 subjects, 17 subjects had all their sampled PK observations as BLQ: all 9 subjects from study VMT-VT-1161-CL-001 at dose levels 5 and 10 mg, 7 patients from study VMT-VT-1161-CL-005 and 1 patient from study VMT-VT-1161-CL-006. All BLQ observations were kept in the analysis dataset and were handled using the M3 method during the popPK analysis.
L1446: The summary statistics for the continuous and categorical covariates are presented by study in Table 146 (individuals in which all PK observations were BLQ were discarded for the summary). A total of 203 adult patients with acute vulvovaginal candidiasis and recurrent vulvovaginal candidiasis were available for the popPK analysis.

L1468: ## Base PK model
L1469: The pharmacokinetics of oteseconazole was best described by a 2-compartment distribution model with linear elimination from the central compartment. Absorption was characterized as first-order with a lag time. The PK model was parameterized in terms of apparent clearance (CL/F), apparent central and peripheral volumes of distribution (V2/F and V3/F respectively), apparent intercompartmental clearance (Q/F), absorption rate constant (Ka) and absorption lag time. Between subject variability was estimated on CL/F, V2/F, V3/F and Ka. The residual error model consisted of a combined additive and proportional error model.
L1470: ## Covariates Analysis
L1471: Age, gender, race, body weight, body mass index (BMI), health status (healthy volunteer, VVC, onychomycosis), diet (fasted, regular, or high-fat meal), formulation (capsules, over-encapsulated tablets, tablets without over-encapsulation), total bilirubin, albumin, alanine aminotransferase (ALT), aspartate aminotransferase (AST), estimated glomerular filtration (using the modification of diet in renal disease [MDRD] formula) were the examined covariates during covariate model selection.
L1472: The following covariates were included in the final model:
L1473: - Body Weight (time varying covariate) on CL/F, Q/F, V2/F and V3/F using a power model, centered at 70 kg
- Race (white versus all other groups) on CL/F (using an exponential model)
- Total Bilirubin (time varying covariate) on CL/F using a power model, centered at a median total serum bilirubin level of 0.5 mg/dL
- Albumin (time varying covariate) on V2/F using a power model, centered at a median albumin level of 4.4 g/dL
- Formulation (capsules and non-over-encapsulated tablets) on the relative oral bioavailability (F1)
- Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-002 on V2/F (using an exponential model)
- Study VMT-VT-1161-CL-007 on Ka (using an exponential model)
L1474: ## Final Model
L1475: The parameter estimates from the final popPK model for oteseconazole are listed in Table 147. According to the popPK model, an average 70 kg white individual with a normal total bilirubin level of 0.5 mg/dL, normal albumin level of 4.4 g/dL, and receiving the capsules formulation, the population estimates for oteseconazole CL/F, V2/F, Q/F, V3/F, F1, Ka and absorption lag time are 0.253 L/h (6.07 L/day), 423 L, 44.5 L/h, 894 L, 1.9 (i.e., 90% higher bioavailability compared to over-encapsulated tablets), 0.278 h-1 and 0.956 h. The estimated interindividual or between subject variability expressed as coefficient of variation (%CV) was 155%, 53%, 56% and 334% for CL/F, V2/F, V3/F, and Ka, respectively. The interindividual random effect (ETA) shrinkage from the final popPK model was 14.8%, 42.3%, 16.6%, and 43.3% for CL/F, V2/F, V3/F, and Ka.
